<a id="item-189"></a>
## [OTAP: Optimal Transport Metric for Evaluating LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17082) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces OTAP (Optimal Transport for Agentic Planning), a metric that reframes trajectory evaluation as a distance between the agent's execution graph and a set of valid solution graphs, instantiated via an unbalanced fused Gromov-Wasserstein transport problem over attributed dependency graphs. On controlled perturbations and three public benchmarks, OTAP separates valid from invalid trajectories where semantics-only metrics score below chance, and a blinded study of 236 trajectories finds it has the strongest agreement among six compared metrics. Current evaluation of LLM agents relies on binary success flags or exact matching, which cannot distinguish sound solutions from lucky ones or penalize valid plans that are reordered or decomposed differently. OTAP provides a more nuanced, structure-aware score that could improve how agent planning and execution quality is measured, especially in open-ended domains where no formal verifier exists. The idealized globally minimized entropy-free objective is invariant to dependency-preserving relabelings of a fixed graph, and unbalanced marginals handle missing or hallucinated steps without forcing a match. However, granularity invariance remains an open problem, and controlled edge-noise experiments reveal sensitivity to dependency extraction; where a formal verifier exists, strict surface metrics predict validity better than OTAP.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents solve tasks by generating trajectories that interleave planning, tool calls, and intermediate results, often represented as dependency graphs where nodes are steps and edges capture prerequisites. Optimal transport is a mathematical framework for comparing probability distributions, and the Gromov-Wasserstein variant extends it to compare structured objects like graphs by aligning their internal distance matrices. The fused unbalanced variant combines feature matching with relaxed marginal constraints, making it suitable for noisy or incomplete graph comparisons.

## [Pluralistic Alignment Research Lacks Adoption by Frontier Labs, Audit Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22305) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.22305v2) audits major frontier labs and open-weight developers and finds no public evidence that pluralistic value alignment has shaped how their models are trained or evaluated. The authors identify three barriers to adoption and propose a research roadmap to address each. This paper highlights a significant gap between responsible AI research and actual practice, showing that even active research agendas like pluralistic alignment may not influence deployed systems used by billions. It matters for AI ethics researchers, policymakers, and anyone concerned about AI systems representing diverse human values. The three barriers are: (1) justifications for pluralistic alignment are mostly normative or theoretical, requiring empirical studies on benefits and drawbacks; (2) the research community has not settled when pluralistic behavior is warranted or what it should look like in practice; (3) existing evaluations and methods are not ready for deployment and need to report effects on other model properties. The authors note these barriers are not unique to pluralistic alignment and the roadmap applies to other responsible AI areas.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Pluralistic value alignment is a research agenda aimed at building AI systems that represent and serve diverse human values and perspectives, rather than a single monolithic set of values. It has emerged as part of responsible AI research, alongside efforts like fairness, transparency, and safety. Frontier labs are leading AI developers that train the largest, most capable models, while open-weight developers release models whose parameters are publicly available.

## [Agent Gym: Continuous Evaluation and Evolution of LLM Agents via Human-in-the-Loop Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15591) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Agent Gym is a modular, domain-agnostic framework that wraps any existing LLM-based agent in a continuous evaluation-and-evolution loop, enabling post-deployment behavioral correction without modifying the agent's source code. It provides six composable capabilities—Act, Evaluate, Investigate, Correct, Learn, and Observe—organized across a constitution layer, a runtime inference pipeline, and a learning loop, with an open-source reference implementation for invoice processing. Production LLM agents are frozen at deployment time while business rules and edge cases keep evolving, and existing approaches require heavy log collection and manual re-engineering that negates the economic value of agentic transformation. Agent Gym addresses this by letting subject matter experts discover and validate correction rules through natural language, making continuous agent maintenance cheaper and more practical for enterprises. The framework's technical contributions include a hybrid deterministic-LLM correction engine with 21 condition operators and three-tier actions, a three-layer investigation architecture for ground-truth-free compliance validation, and a programmatic safety loop that guarantees rule correctness before human approval. It also introduces the Spec-to-Note Gap, an autoencoder-inspired view of agentic system transparency, though as an arXiv preprint it lacks independent validation or community discussion so far.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents are AI systems that use a large language model to plan and execute multi-step tasks, often in business workflows such as invoice processing. In traditional software, behavior can be patched after deployment, but LLM agents are typically frozen once deployed, so handling new edge cases requires engineering teams to collect logs, re-examine designs, and redeploy. Human-in-the-loop feedback means domain experts review agent outputs and provide corrections, which Agent Gym turns into reusable rules that the agent applies at runtime.

## [Cognitive capability profiles match AI agents to workplace tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25623) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.25623v2) introduces a pipeline that profiles AI agents and workplace tasks using a shared set of core cognitive capabilities, then combines them to estimate AI suitability at the domain, organization, role, or individual duty level. The authors validate capability recovery on synthetic agents, profile six AI systems, and elicit task requirements from 410 employees across six occupational domains. Aggregate benchmark scores offer little insight into where AI systems will succeed or fail in real work, and human judgments of model capabilities quickly become outdated. This framework gives organizations a comparative scoping tool to identify promising candidates for piloting and areas where current systems are unlikely to be well suited, potentially improving AI deployment decisions. The pipeline uses a benchmark battery annotated for the cognitive demands of each item to infer agent capabilities, and elicits from domain experts the relative importance of the same capabilities for their work. The authors found that AI systems differed more across cognitive dimensions than across model families, while workplace activities converged on a shared cognitive core; they also discuss extending the framework to profile human workers for human-machine task allocation.

## [CivBench: A Long-Horizon MCP Benchmark for LLM Agents in Civilization VI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02459) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released CivBench, an open-source benchmark that evaluates language model agents in Civilization VI through 76 Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools, with episodes spanning over 300 turns and thousands of tool calls. The pilot study across four model families and 23 admissible runs introduces two interface-level metrics: Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR) and RAG@10, which measures whether commitments in planning reflections are executed within ten subsequent turns. CivBench addresses a gap in agent evaluation by testing sustained planning, state monitoring, and commitment execution in a long-horizon, partially observable, tool-mediated setting rather than short task episodes. Its open-source environment, logs, and metrics give agent researchers a reusable testbed for studying instruction-following failures that aggregate win rates alone cannot reveal. The authors explicitly caution that the 23-run sample is a pilot, not a model ranking, since aggregate outcomes do not reliably discriminate between models at this scale. Under a shared playbook protocol, agents queried victory progress only every 30–75 turns despite guidance to do so every 20 turns, and RAG@10 ranged from 48.2% to 65.8% across models, which the authors interpret as deviations under instruction rather than absent capability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 that standardizes how AI applications connect to external tools and data sources. Long-horizon agent planning refers to agents that must execute extended sequences of decisions and tool calls to reach complex goals, a setting where failures in monitoring and follow-through are common. Civilization VI provides a naturally long, partially observable strategy game that stresses these capabilities.

## [CARE: Context-Adaptive Rubrics for Empathetic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34249) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Context-Adaptive Rubric Evolution (CARE), which generates a per-turn rubric that reweights cognitive, affective, and proactive empathy dimensions and adjusts fine-grained evaluation criteria, serving as an adaptive reward interface for online empathetic RL. Integrated with RLVER and MICA, CARE achieves state-of-the-art results on SentientBench, EQBench3, and EMPA under three independent LLM judges, improving EMPA's EPM-Idx from 28.11 to 83.54 under Gemini-2.5-Pro. This addresses a fundamental mismatch in empathetic RL: support priorities shift as a dialogue unfolds, yet existing methods optimize a fixed reward specification across all turns. By making the reward itself context-adaptive, CARE could improve multi-turn emotional-support agents and influence how reward modeling is designed for other evolving, state-dependent alignment tasks. The rubric generator is trained with turn-level rubric supervision and human preference data via supervised fine-tuning followed by preference-based reinforcement learning (GRPO), then used to reward policy responses during online GRPO. Analyses show learned rubric priorities vary systematically across dialogue stages and user emotions, though results depend on LLM judges and the specific RLVER/MICA integration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Empathetic dialogue systems aim to respond with appropriate emotional understanding, and reinforcement learning from human or AI feedback is increasingly used to align LLMs for this purpose. Prior work such as RLVER uses verifiable emotion rewards from simulated users, while MICA targets multi-turn credit assignment in emotional support dialogues. Rubrics—structured checklists of evaluation criteria—are commonly used to score open-ended responses, but typically remain fixed throughout a conversation.

## [AutoBCI: Agentic Neural Architecture Discovery for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35456) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoBCI introduces a two-agent framework in which a Designer Agent performs Pool-Guided Architecture Discovery (PGAD) and a Forecaster Agent performs Performance Estimation from Early Knowledge (PEEK) to discover and select EEG decoding architectures. Evaluated across 14 EEG datasets with six LLMs, the architecture found using Claude Opus 5.5 achieved 64.16% average test balanced accuracy, slightly surpassing the strongest baseline REVE at 63.87%. This work shows that LLM-driven agentic search can automate architecture design for brain-computer interfaces, potentially reducing the need for hand-crafted EEG models across heterogeneous tasks. The early-performance forecasting component could also cut the computational cost of architecture search by pruning unpromising candidates before full training. Using only ten observed training epochs, PEEK reduced the mean absolute error in predicting average validation balanced accuracy from 2.20 to 1.36 percentage points, a 38.1% reduction over the best-observed-score baseline. The comparison includes six conventional EEG models and four foundation models, and code is released on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: EEG-based brain-computer interfaces record electrical brain activity non-invasively and rely on decoding models to translate signals into commands or predictions for tasks such as motor imagery, emotion recognition, and sleep staging. Neural architecture search (NAS) automates the design of neural networks, and recent agentic AI frameworks use large language models to plan and execute multi-step tasks autonomously. AutoBCI combines these ideas by letting LLM agents propose, train, and forecast EEG decoding architectures.

## [MEA: Reward-Driven Multi-Agent System for Faithful Model Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02480) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present MEA, a multi-agent framework in which a Proposer agent selects and configures post-hoc explanation tools based on the question and modality, while an Actor agent is optimized end-to-end against faithfulness to generate natural language explanations. The system is evaluated across tabular, text, and vision modalities on six datasets, with reward-driven optimization yielding faithfulness gains of +28%, +21%, and +34% over the untrained backbone, respectively. MEA addresses the expertise barrier that prevents domain experts from effectively using post-hoc explanation tools, and its finding that frontier LLMs systematically produce unfaithful explanations highlights a critical limitation of current LLM-based explainability. By showing that AI agents can serve as a scalable, adaptable interface to ML explainability, the work opens a path toward natural-language explanations that generalize beyond fixed, single-purpose tools. The framework introduces diverse question types spanning feature attribution, counterfactual reasoning, and spurious feature detection, each paired with a perturbation-based faithfulness metric. A modality-adaptive penalty is added to the faithfulness reward, and MEA consistently outperforms post-hoc explainers, agentic, and closed-source baselines across six datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Post-hoc explanation methods are techniques that attribute the outputs of black-box machine learning models to input features after training, providing local transparency. Feature attribution assigns importance scores to input features, while counterfactual explanations show how inputs would need to change to alter a prediction. Faithfulness metrics evaluate whether explanations actually reflect the factors that influence a model's prediction, and prior work has shown that post-hoc methods cannot guarantee the insights they generate.

## [MASBench: New Benchmark for LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from BUPT-GAMMA have released MASBench, a benchmark that evaluates LLM-based multi-agent collaboration mechanisms under partial observability constraints. It organizes tasks into three progressive categories—Reasoning, Scheduling, and Game—to systematically test Protocol, Memory, and Routing collaboration mechanisms, with deterministic metrics for performance, communication cost, and cost effectiveness. Most existing multi-agent benchmarks assume global observability, which does not reflect real-world constraints where agents only access partial information due to physical or privacy limits. MASBench fills this gap by providing a systematic way to evaluate how collaboration mechanisms bridge information gaps, offering empirical guidance for designing more realistic and effective multi-agent systems. The benchmark evaluates three representative collaboration mechanisms (Protocol, Memory, Routing) across three task categories, and reports deterministic metrics including performance score, communication cost, and cost effectiveness. Experiments span diverse LLM backbones and mechanism configurations, and the code is available at https://github.com/BUPT-GAMMA/MASBench.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based multi-agent systems coordinate multiple LLM agents to solve complex tasks that exceed individual capabilities, and their effectiveness depends heavily on collaboration mechanisms. Partial observability, a core challenge in multi-agent systems formalized by decentralized partially observable Markov decision processes (Dec-POMDPs), means each agent only sees a limited view of the environment. While prior work has studied partial observability in reinforcement learning and surveyed collaboration mechanisms in LLM-based MAS, few benchmarks systematically evaluate collaboration under such constraints.

<a id="item-198"></a>
## [RetryBench Separates Retry Permission from Recovery Outcomes in LLM Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15884) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RetryBench introduces an evaluation protocol that scores retry decisions in tool-calling LLM agents on retry permission, settled effects, and completion by replaying each decision across all hidden execution worlds consistent with the observation. In two prompt-sensitivity studies (1,728 decisions on four synthetic software-engineering tasks), GPT-5.6 Luna reused a valid idempotency key in only 10/40 decisions when an extra key was described as a "Candidate recovery key" but in 39/40 when it was called an "Alternative key string". This work cleanly separates whether a retry is permitted from whether recovery ultimately succeeds, offering agent-reliability researchers a principled way to measure failure handling rather than just task success. The striking prompt-sensitivity results show that model retry behavior can hinge on superficial wording, which matters for anyone deploying tool-using agents in production where duplicate side effects are costly. The protocol replays each decision across all hidden execution worlds consistent with the observation, and the second study found that listing retry first lowered retry rates by 17–27 points for GPT-5.6 Terra and GPT-5.4 under underspecified lookup guarantees, an option-order bias the authors do not judge as better or worse. Findings are limited to researcher-chosen phrasings, four tasks, and unpinned model aliases, though code and all model responses are released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents that call external tools must decide what to do when a tool call times out: retrying risks duplicate side effects, while not retrying can leave the task incomplete. Idempotency keys are a standard API mechanism that lets a client safely retry a mutation by ensuring the server performs the side effect only once, so a valid key is what makes a retry permissible. RetryBench builds on this concept by scoring whether a model's retry is justified by an idempotency-key guarantee or proof of non-execution, rather than only checking whether the task was completed.

## [SCAMPER: Dual-prior model for realistic rodent motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27279) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SCAMPER (Simulated Control via Augmented Motor Priors for Embodied Reinforcement learning), a variational architecture with two distinct priors—a motion prior for control and a behavior prior for exploration—to train an embodied virtual rat. The system produces natural movements during both goal-directed behavior and random exploration, a capability not achieved by prior embodied animal models. This work addresses a key gap in neuro-AI by enabling embodied models that move realistically in both trained and exploratory contexts, which is essential for making valid comparisons between biological and artificial agents. It also allows learning from sparse rewards, making it suitable for modeling realistic neuroscience experiments. SCAMPER combines a motion prior for control and a behavior prior for exploration within a variational framework, and the diversity induced by the behavior prior enables learning from sparse rewards. The system is designed to model realistic sparse-reward tasks from experimental neuroscience.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Embodied models of animal behavior aim to create virtual agents that mimic real animals, allowing direct comparison between biological and artificial systems. Prior work has developed virtual animal bodies and imitation-based training for realistic motion, but no system produced natural movements during both goal-directed behavior and random exploration. SCAMPER builds on variational methods and reinforcement learning to fill this gap.

## [OS-Wizard scales computer-using agents to specialized software via knowledge-guided exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33907) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OS-Wizard introduces a knowledge-guided automated trajectory collection framework that combines tutorial-guided exploration with a hybrid GUI-Code action space and tree-based checkpointing. It collects 10.9K automatically validated trajectories and fine-tunes open-source base models, achieving 64.1% accuracy on a specialized desktop workflow benchmark—a 58.2 percentage point improvement and a new state of the art. This addresses a key bottleneck in computer-using agent research: the scarcity of high-quality interaction trajectories for specialized desktop applications. By automating trajectory collection and showing that the resulting supervision also improves general desktop tasks, the work could accelerate the development of agents for niche professional software. The framework uses a hybrid action space that allows direct execution of application-native commands alongside GUI actions, simplifying complex workflows. Tree exploration with checkpointing and revisiting of pivotal states enables broader coverage of alternative workflows beyond linear rollouts, and ablations confirm the value of code actions in specialized workflows.

**Background**: Computer-using agents are AI systems that interact with digital environments—desktops, mobile devices, or web platforms—through graphical user interfaces, typically by perceiving screenshots and issuing clicks or keystrokes. Supervised fine-tuning on annotated interaction trajectories is a common way to improve their task-solving abilities, but such data is scarce for specialized desktop software due to limited application knowledge and complex GUI workflows. A hybrid GUI-Code action space lets agents combine low-level visual interactions with high-level programmatic commands, which can make automation more efficient and robust.

## [AutoEnergyDesign: LLM agent optimizes energy system design with git-based keep/revert](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35160) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoEnergyDesign introduces an LLM-driven agent that iteratively proposes and reverts energy system design configurations using simulation-grounded feedback to minimize 25-year net present cost. In a case study with measured building loads, the agent, instantiated with three Claude models (Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5), reached an NPC of 3.53 MEUR, which is 1.20% below the best result of TPE Bayesian optimization and 0.23% below the best of a (1+1) evolution strategy. This work demonstrates that LLM agents with a simple keep/revert mechanism can outperform established black-box optimizers like TPE and evolution strategies on a complex, simulation-heavy engineering design problem, achieving near-optimal solutions in far fewer simulations. It suggests a promising direction for AI-assisted engineering design where reasoning models interact with simulators to navigate high-dimensional, discrete-continuous search spaces. The agent couples a reasoning model with explicit memories, a constrained action interface, and an environment-grounded keep/revert feedback signal, with every decision recorded for auditability. The simulator covers eight technologies and computes the 25-year NPC; the agent came within 1% of the best result after a median of 18 to 27.5 simulations, while among baselines only the evolution strategy did so, after a median of 192 evaluations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Building energy system design optimization is a high-dimensional search problem involving discrete technology choices, continuous sizing parameters, and economic trade-offs, where each candidate design must be evaluated by a year-long simulation. Net Present Cost (NPC) is a standard metric that sums all lifetime costs and revenues of an energy system, discounted to present value, and is widely used in techno-economic analysis of renewable and hybrid energy systems. LLM agents are increasingly applied to simulation-based optimization, often using feedback mechanisms to refine tool calls or design proposals before real-world execution.

## [MedConstBench: Benchmarking How Constitutions Steer LLMs in Medical Ethics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36513) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MedConstBench introduces a benchmark that treats the deployment constitution as a controlled variable to evaluate how it steers LLM behavior in medical ethical dilemmas, organized around Beauchamp's four principles and scored by human-calibrated multi-model peer review. It includes MedDilemma, a new dataset of clinical dilemmas, and evaluates ten open-source LLMs under four constitutions, finding that constitution effects are dimension-selective and model-dependent with no universally optimal constitution. This work addresses an underexplored area by treating ethics as a variable rather than a fixed model property, which is valuable for AI safety and alignment research in medical AI deployment. It provides a principled framework and a practical adaptive selector that could inform how institutions choose constitutions for locally deployed medical LLMs. The benchmark uses Beauchamp's four principles (autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, and justice) to structure evaluation and employs human-calibrated multi-model peer review for scoring. The proposed do-no-harm adaptive selector chooses a constitution per model, matches the per-model oracle, guarantees a worst-case floor, and generalizes to unseen models at low cost.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Beauchamp and Childress's four principles of biomedical ethics—respect for autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, and justice—are widely used as a framework for moral reasoning in healthcare. Constitutional AI refers to approaches where a written set of principles (a constitution) guides an LLM's behavior, and once a model is deployed locally, its ethical policy is mainly governed by that constitution. LLM-as-a-judge and peer review methods use models to evaluate other models' outputs, often calibrated against human judgments to improve reliability.

AuroraWorld proposes a structured 4D world modeling framework that synthesizes physically consistent egocentric robot manipulation videos from large-scale exocentric footage, addressing viewpoint transformation, severe occlusion, complex object interactions, and sparse exocentric observations. The framework is explicitly designed to handle substantial viewpoint transformations, severe occlusions, complex object interactions, and inherently sparse observations from available exocentric viewpoints, which are the failure modes of prior exo-to-ego synthesis methods. As a single arXiv paper with no community discussion yet, its quantitative results and generalization remain to be validated by independent reproduction.

## [New Metrics Quantify Local and Global Fidelity of Nonlinear Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00290) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a DLP curve and chance-corrected local (L) and global (G) fidelity metrics, along with an LG reversal test, to rigorously quantify whether nonlinear embeddings like t-SNE and UMAP preserve local versus global structure compared to PCA. On twelve datasets of up to 1.38 million points, G agrees with established global scores at 0.84 and 0.93, and the area under R_NX at 0.22 and 0.30. This work provides a principled, statistically grounded way to evaluate the long-standing claim that t-SNE and UMAP preserve local structure while PCA preserves global structure, which is widely assumed but rarely quantified. It will benefit machine learning and visualization practitioners who need to choose dimensionality reduction methods and understand their trade-offs. The DLP curve measures the share of each point's k nearest neighbors that a map retains; after chance correction, it yields L at k=10 and G over 30–50% of the data, and at the midpoint the curve is provably a rank correlation of distances. The LG reversal test decides with a stated error rate which of two maps is local and which is global, and an entity fidelity metric E predicts which entities a map loses beyond L and G where entity sizes differ; in an agent memory of one million passages, t-SNE and PCA cross at 150,000 passages (p=2.6e-5).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Dimensionality reduction techniques like t-SNE and UMAP are widely used to visualize high-dimensional data in two or three dimensions, while PCA is a classical linear method. A common belief is that t-SNE and UMAP excel at preserving local neighborhoods, whereas PCA better captures global structure, but this claim has lacked rigorous quantification. The paper addresses this gap by proposing metrics that separate local and global fidelity and by providing a statistical test to compare embeddings.

## [VCoT-SQL validates reasoning steps to boost Text-to-SQL accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00815) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VCoT-SQL introduces a framework that uses intermediate-step validated chain-of-thought supervision and a dedicated SQL validator to improve Text-to-SQL generation. It achieves 66.82% execution accuracy on BIRD dev and 86.8% on Spider test, improving a base 7B model by 7% on BIRD. This approach shows that rigorous step-level validation of synthetic chain-of-thought data can be more effective than scaling up teacher model size, enabling small open-source models to rival or outperform frontier closed-source models. It addresses a key weakness in existing CoT synthesis methods that only verify final answers, potentially improving data quality for fine-tuning. VCoT-SQL trains two complementary models: an SQL generator using clause-level CoT templates with reasoning steps verified via execution and schema alignment checks, and an SQL validator using diagnostic CoT templates to detect syntactic, semantic, and type-level errors. It also uses a hierarchical schema filtering pipeline that integrates retrieval strategies to distill relevant database elements.

Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting is a technique that encourages large language models to produce intermediate reasoning steps, improving performance on complex tasks. However, standard Text-to-SQL datasets lack CoT annotations, so synthetic CoT data is often generated, but existing methods only verify the final answer, allowing flawed reasoning to corrupt training data.

## [AgenticFinBench: New Benchmark for Financial Agents with Hallucination-Aware Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04813) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced AgenticFinBench, a benchmark of 230 real-world financial tasks spanning 8 categories, 2 languages, and 4 complexity levels, along with a reference-free evaluation framework that combines question-specific rubrics and a two-stage claim audit to detect hallucinations. Experiments across 18 agent configurations show that hallucination auditing lowers the baseline leader's score by 7.58 points and moves it from first to fourth place. Financial agents are increasingly deployed for real-world work, but existing benchmarks fail to capture the breadth and open-endedness of financial tasks or separate evidence grounding from answer correctness. This benchmark provides a more realistic evaluation that could drive improvements in financial reasoning, factual verification, and agent design. The framework defines hallucinations as claims absent from the agent's trace and refuted by external evidence, and the study finds that harness differences explain more variation in evidence grounding than model differences (28.6% vs 8.8%). Quality scores also drop from 89.44 on direct retrieval tasks to 79.18 on the most complex tasks, with case studies showing agents struggle to explain how events affect profits or stock prices and to compare financial data consistently.

Hallucination detection in AI agents aims to identify fabricated or unsupported claims, often by decomposing outputs into claims and checking them against evidence. Agentic benchmarks evaluate AI systems as autonomous agents completing multi-step tasks, and financial benchmarks like FinAgentBench have focused on agentic retrieval but not on separating grounding from correctness.

## [ScamWorld: A New Benchmark for Testing Web Agents Against Online Fraud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04879) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ScamWorld, an interactive environment that reconstructs reported scam websites across diverse scenarios, fraud mechanisms, and languages to systematically evaluate how web agents respond to online fraud during task execution. In a study of agents powered by six frontier LLMs, baseline deception rates ranged from 11% to 35%, with agents more likely to proceed with payments than with requests for credentials or wallet information. This work addresses an underexplored safety issue: web agents that browse, shop, and manage accounts on behalf of users can be deceived by real-world online fraud, not just adversarial prompt injections. ScamWorld provides a reusable, controlled testbed that could help developers stress-test and harden agents before deployment, affecting AI safety research and the broader agent ecosystem. Each reconstructed website reproduces documented fraud mechanisms, making relevant signs of fraud observable before the agent's final action and enabling controlled, repeatable evaluation. Analysis of agent trajectories shows that some agents acknowledge suspicious conditions yet proceed, at times treating the user's instructions as authorization to accept additional terms imposed by the website.

## [Learning Principal Incentivizes Learning Agent with Unobserved Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.06717) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a repeated adverse selection game where a learning principal must incentivize a learning agent without observing the agent's reward realizations. The authors propose a non-parametric estimator that uses only the history of incentives and agent choices, and combine it with a data-driven incentive policy within a multi-armed bandit framework, proving finite-sample consistency and a regret bound. This work bridges principal-agent models and multi-armed bandits under information asymmetry, addressing a gap in settings where both parties learn. It has practical relevance for renewable energy storage contracts and personalized healthcare incentives, where principals cannot directly observe agent rewards. The estimator is non-parametric and does not restrict the type of the agent's algorithm, making it broadly applicable. The theoretical guarantees account for the sequential externality imposed by the agent's learning, and simulations on green energy aggregator contracts support the framework.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: In principal-agent models, adverse selection occurs when the agent has private information before contracting, leading to information asymmetry. Multi-armed bandit problems model sequential decision-making under uncertainty, balancing exploration and exploitation. This paper combines these concepts: the agent faces a bandit problem to maximize reward plus incentive, while the principal learns to estimate rewards and design incentives without observing them.

## [EgoGen: Synthetic Data Generator for Egocentric Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.08739) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EgoGen introduces a synthetic data generator that produces accurate ground-truth training data for egocentric perception tasks by simulating human movements and behaviors in 3D environments. At its core is a novel human motion synthesis model that uses egocentric visual inputs to sense the environment, combined with collision-avoiding motion primitives and a two-stage reinforcement learning approach. This work addresses a critical gap in synthetic data for egocentric perception, which is fundamental for augmented reality and embodied AI. By eliminating the need for pre-defined global paths and enabling application to dynamic environments, EgoGen offers a scalable solution that could accelerate research in AR/VR and first-person computer vision. The motion synthesis model offers a closed-loop solution where embodied perception and movement are seamlessly coupled, and it is directly applicable to dynamic environments without pre-defined paths. EgoGen will be fully open-sourced, and its efficacy is demonstrated in three tasks: mapping and localization for head-mounted cameras, egocentric camera tracking, and human mesh recovery from egocentric views.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Egocentric vision, or first-person vision, is a sub-field of computer vision that analyzes images and videos captured by wearable cameras, typically worn on the head or chest, approximating the wearer's visual field. Synthetic data has been widely used to train third-person vision models, but its application to egocentric perception remains largely unexplored due to the challenge of simulating natural human movements that steer embodied cameras to capture faithful first-person views. Human motion synthesis involves generating realistic human movements, often using techniques like motion capture or reinforcement learning, and is crucial for creating such simulations.

## [RADT: Decision Transformer for Offline Reach-Avoid RL with Hindsight Relabeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19337) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RADT, a decision transformer for offline, reward-free, goal-conditioned reinforcement learning that encodes goals and avoid-regions as prompt tokens, enabling flexible specification at evaluation time. Using only suboptimal trajectories from a random policy and a novel avoid-region hindsight relabeling approach, RADT learns reach-avoid behavior without reward or cost supervision. This work removes the need for meticulously designed reward and cost functions and enables zero-shot generalization to out-of-distribution avoid-region sizes and counts, outperforming baselines that require retraining. It could simplify the deployment of safe RL agents in robotics and other safety-critical domains where avoid-regions change dynamically. RADT is benchmarked against existing offline goal-conditioned RL models across 17 tasks, environments, and experimental settings; in one zero-shot setting, it achieves a 35.7% improvement in normalized cost over the best retrained baseline while maintaining high goal-reaching success. The method is also applied to cell reprogramming in biology, demonstrating versatility beyond standard RL benchmarks.

**Background**: Decision transformers recast reinforcement learning as sequence modeling, leveraging Transformer architectures to predict actions from trajectories of states, actions, and returns. Goal-conditioned RL trains agents to reach specified goals, and hindsight relabeling improves sample efficiency by relabeling failed trajectories with goals actually achieved. Reach-avoid tasks require an agent to reach a target while avoiding undesirable regions, and offline RL learns from fixed datasets without online interaction.

## [GO-CBED: Goal-Oriented Bayesian Sequential Design for Causal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07359) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GO-CBED is a new goal-oriented Bayesian framework for sequential causal experimental design that directly maximizes expected information gain (EIG) on user-specified causal quantities, rather than inferring the full causal model. It uses a transformer-based policy network and normalizing flow-based variational posteriors to optimize a variational lower bound on EIG, enabling real-time decision-making via an amortized network. This approach enables more targeted and efficient experimentation by focusing only on causal quantities relevant to the research goal, which can significantly reduce experimental costs. It outperforms existing baselines in synthetic and semi-synthetic tasks, particularly under limited budgets and complex causal mechanisms, making it valuable for fields like genomics and causal discovery. The framework is non-myopic, optimizing over entire intervention sequences, and addresses the intractability of exact EIG computation via a variational lower bound estimator. It was evaluated on synthetic structural causal models and semi-synthetic gene regulatory networks, showing consistent improvements over baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Bayesian experimental design is a decision-theoretic framework that selects experiments by maximizing expected utility, often defined as expected information gain, to reduce uncertainty about model parameters. Causal inference aims to understand cause-effect relationships, and sequential design involves adaptively choosing interventions over multiple steps. Normalizing flows are flexible generative models used to approximate complex posterior distributions in variational inference, while transformers are neural networks adept at sequence modeling.

## [Splice: Gene-Splicing-Inspired Source Selection for ML Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22186) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes source selection as finding a subset of discovered data sources that maximizes downstream task utility, and proposes Splice, a target- and cost-aware system that uses gene splicing to identify complementary sources. It also introduces two surrogate variants, Splice-DP and Splice-Grad, which estimate marginal contributions via meta-learning data profiles and gradient alignment, achieving 3-7x fewer model evaluations (up to 77x at scale) while matching quality. Existing data lake discovery techniques find related sources via schema similarity and joinability, but not all are useful and some may introduce harmful noise or distribution shifts. Splice improves binary F1 by 3-5 percentage points over using all discovered tables and boosts task utility by 1.8-13.7 points across five real-world ML tasks, directly benefiting data lake and ML communities. Splice adopts the idea of gene splicing to identify sources providing additional signals that the union of all sources misses. The surrogate variants leverage data profiles in a meta-learning approach and gradient alignment, respectively, to estimate marginal contributions of subsets to task utility, enabling scalability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Data lakes contain many tables, and discovery tools find sources related to a query table through schema similarity and joinability. However, not all discovered sources are useful for downstream analyses; some may introduce noise or distribution shifts. Gene splicing is a biological process where introns are removed and exons are joined to produce mature RNA, inspiring Splice's approach to select complementary data sources. Surrogate models are approximations that speed up expensive evaluations, and gradient alignment is a meta-learning technique that aligns gradients to improve transfer.

## [Benchmark Finds Neural Molecular Embeddings Rarely Beat ECFP Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06199) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale benchmark evaluated 28 pretrained molecular embedding models across 25 classification datasets using hierarchical Bayesian Bradley-Terry statistical testing. All neural models except CheMeleon failed to outperform the classical ECFP count fingerprint, and even CheMeleon's advantage disappeared under stricter external validation with scaffold cross-validation and a Bayesian hierarchical correlated t-test. This rigorous comparison challenges the prevailing assumption that increasingly sophisticated neural architectures automatically yield better molecular representations, and it suggests that practitioners in drug discovery and QSAR/QSPR modeling should not abandon classical fingerprints without evidence. The findings highlight the need for standardized, statistically sound evaluation in molecular machine learning. The study recommends that new QSAR/QSPR studies evaluate both ECFP fingerprints and CheMeleon embeddings as baselines. The authors note that the results challenge perceived progress in molecular representation learning and call for rigorous evaluation standards, though the benchmark is limited to classification datasets and does not cover regression tasks or other molecular property prediction settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Molecular representation learning aims to encode chemical structures as vectors that machine learning models can use to predict properties such as toxicity or bioactivity. Extended-Connectivity Fingerprints (ECFPs) are a classical, circular topological fingerprint method widely used for similarity searching and structure-activity modeling. Pretrained molecular embedding models, including graph neural networks and text transformers, have been proposed as more expressive alternatives, but large-scale rigorous comparisons of their representations were lacking.

A new arXiv paper (2509.10127v3) proposes a systematic framework that uses LLMs to generate narrative personas from long-term social media data, filters them for quality, and then applies importance sampling to align the persona set with reference psychometric distributions such as the Big Five personality traits. The framework also includes a task-specific module that adapts the globally aligned set to targeted subpopulations for specific simulation contexts. This work addresses a key bottleneck in LLM-based social simulation: unrepresentative persona sets introduce population-level bias that undermines the validity of simulation results. By providing a method to generate population-aligned personas, it could improve the fidelity of computational social science research and policy applications that rely on LLM agents. The method combines LLM-generated narrative personas from social media data with importance sampling—a Monte Carlo technique for reweighting samples from a proposal distribution to match a target distribution—to achieve global alignment with psychometric references. A task-specific adaptation module further tailors the globally aligned set to subpopulations, and experiments show significant reduction in population-level bias.

## [SPS-GAN learns interpretable phase-space structure from trajectories and video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23003) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SPS-GAN is a conditional generative framework that combines adversarial generation with port-Hamiltonian latent dynamics and extends cyclic-coordinate regularization to handle dissipation and forcing, enabling discovery of interpretable latent phase-space structure from Cartesian trajectories or video observations. Experiments on simulated and real-world systems show improved trajectory accuracy and video generation quality over competitive baselines, while identifying active latent dimensions consistent with expected effective degrees of freedom. This work advances physics-informed machine learning by enabling interpretable latent structure discovery from raw observations across conservative, dissipative, and forced systems, which could benefit robotics, video understanding, and scientific modeling where underlying coordinates are unknown. It extends prior generative approaches like Hamiltonian GAN to broader dynamical regimes and conditional generation across systems. The method uses port-Hamiltonian latent dynamics to guarantee passivity and stability, and extends cyclic-coordinate regularization to account for dissipation and forcing, encouraging compact latent representations from which active degrees of freedom can be identified. It recovers physically interpretable coordinates across observation modalities and dynamical regimes, though the approach is niche and lacks community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Hamiltonian systems describe mechanical dynamics in phase space, where coordinates and momenta evolve according to energy conservation, and port-Hamiltonian systems extend this to include dissipation and external forcing while preserving passivity. Cyclic-coordinate regularization encourages latent representations to be compact and interpretable by penalizing dependence on certain coordinates, and generative adversarial networks (GANs) are used to learn distributions of trajectories or video frames. Prior work like Hamiltonian GAN introduced cyclic-coordinate regularization for conservative systems but did not handle conditional generation across systems or dissipative/forced dynamics.

## [Survey Maps Reasoning-Based Backdoor Attacks in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07697) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2510.07697v2) presents the first comprehensive survey of reasoning-based backdoor attacks in large language models, proposing a unified taxonomy that categorizes these attacks as associative, passive, and active. The survey analyzes underlying mechanisms, methodological frameworks, defense strategies, and unresolved challenges in this emerging security area. As LLMs increasingly rely on advanced reasoning capabilities, adversaries can exploit these capabilities to implant backdoors that are harder to detect than traditional trigger-based attacks. This survey provides AI security researchers and practitioners with a structured framework for understanding and defending against reasoning-level threats, which could influence future secure LLM development. The taxonomy distinguishes associative, passive, and active reasoning-based backdoors, with passive attacks noted as depending on carefully engineered triggers and access to training or customization datasets. The survey also reviews defense strategies and discusses open challenges, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review and lacks community discussion.

**Background**: Backdoor attacks in machine learning involve injecting malicious behavior into a model so that it behaves normally on regular inputs but produces attacker-specified outputs when a specific trigger is present. In LLMs, such attacks can be introduced through data poisoning, weight poisoning, or manipulation of hidden states, and recent work like BackdoorLLM has benchmarked these threats. Reasoning-based backdoors extend this concept by exploiting the model's chain-of-thought or multi-step reasoning processes, making detection more difficult.

Researchers introduced an uncertainty-aware active exploration framework that autonomously learns task-agnostic, generalizable dynamics models for soft robots. Evaluated on three simulated platforms (a continuum arm, an articulated fish in fluid, and a musculoskeletal leg) and a real-world pneumatically actuated continuum arm, the method outperformed random exploration and task-specific baselines in modeling accuracy and zero-shot control. Soft robots are difficult to model due to their compliant, high-dimensional, and nonlinear dynamics, and this work shows that uncertainty-driven active exploration can efficiently collect informative data to build generalizable models. This enables data-efficient dynamics learning across morphologies, supporting autonomous and adaptable control for compliant robots in unstructured environments. The framework actively selects data by leveraging uncertainty estimates, leading to higher modeling accuracy than random exploration and task-specific baselines. Improved model quality translates to higher returns in zero-shot control on unseen tasks and robust performance under sensing noise, actuation delays, and material nonlinearities.

## [AEGIS: First Adversarial Framework to Protect 3D Gaussian Avatar Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17747) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AEGIS is the first framework to apply adversarial perturbations to the base (DC) color coefficients of 3D Gaussian primitives, embedding identity protection directly into the 3D representation so it applies across all viewpoints and animations without retraining or modifying geometry. Evaluated against black-box face recognizers, it reduces face retrieval and verification accuracy to near-zero while maintaining high perceptual fidelity and preserving attributes like age, gender, and emotion. As photorealistic 3D avatars become widespread, they introduce new risks of identity theft and unauthorized biometric tracking. AEGIS addresses a critical gap in viewpoint-consistent privacy protection for dynamic 3D avatars, offering a practical defense that could be integrated into avatar creation pipelines to safeguard users' biometric privacy. The method perturbs only the DC color coefficients guided by an ensemble of pre-trained face verification networks, leaving geometry and other attributes intact. The authors also introduce a viewpoint-robust leakage metric to quantify residual identity leakage across rendered viewpoints, which single-view evaluation cannot capture.

Face verification networks are deep learning models that compare facial images to determine identity, and they are vulnerable to such perturbations.

## [CompilerKV: Offline-Compiled Risk-Adaptive KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08686) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CompilerKV introduces an offline-compiled, risk-adaptive KV retention policy that replaces online per-prompt estimation with portable precomputed tables, reducing online correction to O(1) lookups plus a budget clamp. At a 512-token budget, it achieves compressed-SOTA on four backbones, improving over the strongest prefill-only baseline by +1.67 points on average. This work shows that corrective signals for KV retention exhibit high cross-prompt regularity, enabling offline compilation and even direct model-to-model table transfer with minimal performance loss. This could significantly reduce online overhead and improve the practicality of KV cache compression for long-context LLM inference. Compiled retention tables act as portable architectural priors: rankings transfer across disjoint corpora on four backbones with mean Spearman ρ=0.90, and direct model-to-model table transfer costs only 0.4–0.8 LongBench points on average. Under a fixed 512/32k cache ratio, CompilerKV remains the strongest compressed method through 128k RULER (~73 vs. FullKV ~79, SnapKV ~38), and on 32k NIAH it reaches 0.89 vs. SnapKV 0.42.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: KV cache stores key-value pairs from the attention mechanism to avoid recomputation during autoregressive decoding, but its memory footprint grows with context length and batch size. <a id="item-221"></a>
## [Human Preferences Differ Between Text and Audio, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22710) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled cross-modal study (arXiv:2602.22710) compared human preference annotations for identical semantic content across 100 prompts in text and audio, finding that achieving good agreement requires about 9 raters per modality (ICC(2,k) ≈ .81 for audio, .74 for text) and that cross-modal agreement is near chance. Audio raters showed narrower decision thresholds, reduced length bias, and more user-oriented criteria than text raters. The findings challenge the common assumption that text-based evaluation protocols can be directly applied to speech in preference-based reinforcement learning and AI alignment, suggesting that audio preference data needs modality-specific design and that text-only alignment pipelines may misrepresent human preferences for speech systems. The study uses intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) analysis to quantify inter-rater reliability and shows that synthetic ratings can effectively predict inter-rater agreement, serving as an early signal for stimulus selection and a proxy for human annotations. Code, data, and the audio corpus are publicly released on Hugging Face.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) trains AI systems using human comparisons between outputs rather than explicit numeric rewards, and it is a dominant framework for aligning models with human preferences. However, its evaluation protocols were originally designed for text, and it was unclear whether they generalize to speech. The intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) is a standard statistic for measuring agreement among raters, and this study applies it to compare text and audio preference judgments for the first time in a controlled setting.

## [Harder Tasks Yield Sparser LLM Representations, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03415) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.03415v4) demonstrates that as task difficulty or distribution shift increases, the last hidden states of large language models become systematically sparser at the dataset level. The authors introduce Sparsity-Guided Curriculum In-Context Learning (SG-ICL), which uses sparsity to select few-shot demonstrations matched to query difficulty and outperforms standard CoT and Auto-CoT baselines on MATH-500. This finding links out-of-distribution difficulty to a measurable internal signal, offering a new tool for interpretability and OOD detection in LLMs. It also suggests a practical way to adapt inference strategies based on representational sparsity, potentially improving performance on challenging tasks. The sparsity–difficulty relation is observed across diverse models and domains, and controlled learning-dynamics experiments indicate that representational density is a learned property of data familiarity. The source code is available on GitHub, and the method is evaluated on MATH-500 against CoT and Auto-CoT baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models process inputs through layers of neural networks, and the final hidden state is a high-dimensional vector that summarizes the input for the next-token prediction. Sparsity in representations means that only a small fraction of neurons or dimensions are active, which is often linked to efficient coding and familiarity. Out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs are those that differ from the training distribution, and models often struggle with them. This paper connects these concepts by showing that OOD or difficult inputs lead to sparser representations.

## [Vision models respond to pareidolia but not like humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03989) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.03989v4) tests six vision models, including a vision-only ViT, CLIP, and generative vision-language models, on the FacesInThings dataset to probe face pareidolia. It finds that CLIP ViT-L/14 and two generative models label roughly half of illusory face regions as 'Human' when categories are described as kinds of face, while face detectors rarely detect them and human annotators read them as non-human. The findings show that a 'Human' answer from a vision model is not reliable evidence of a face, revealing deep differences in how models and humans interpret ambiguous visual evidence. This has implications for interpretability, robustness, and the trustworthiness of vision-language models in safety-critical applications. The study uses controlled comparisons: each face-like region is paired with three controls (background crop, tile-shuffled region, and average color) to test whether the 'Human' answer depends on the face-like arrangement. Results vary widely across models—LLaVA-1.5 answers 'Human' less often once the arrangement is removed, Qwen2-VL calls every average-color patch 'Human', CLIP ViT-B/32 calls shuffled regions 'Human' more often than real faces, and SmolVLM rarely answers 'Human' but mostly for face-like regions; rewording categories also changes preferences.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Face pareidolia is the tendency to perceive faces in random or ambiguous stimuli, such as electrical outlets or clouds. It provides a controlled way to study how vision models turn weak visual evidence into category judgments. CLIP is a vision-language model that maps images and text to a shared vector space for zero-shot classification, while generative vision-language models like LLaVA and Qwen2-VL can answer open-ended questions about images. The FacesInThings dataset is a PyTorch dataset designed for studying pareidolia in computer vision.

## [MemReward: Graph-Based Experience Memory for Semi-Supervised LLM RL Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19310) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MemReward introduces a graph-based experience memory that propagates reward signals from labeled to unlabeled rollouts using a heterogeneous graph and a graph neural network (GNN), enabling semi-supervised reinforcement learning fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs). With ground-truth rewards on only 20% of rollouts, it achieves 96.6% of fully supervised performance on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 97.3% on Qwen2.5-3B across 13 benchmarks. This work addresses a key bottleneck in reinforcement learning fine-tuning of LLMs: the scarcity of reward labels. By effectively utilizing unlabeled rollouts, MemReward could significantly reduce annotation costs and make RL-based fine-tuning more accessible for reasoning, question answering, and code generation tasks. MemReward constructs a heterogeneous graph with queries and rollouts (thinking processes and answers) as nodes, connected by similarity and structural edges, and warms up a GNN on labeled rollouts to predict rewards for unlabeled ones during online policy optimization. It outperforms label propagation and FixMatch-style self-training, and generalizes to Llama backbones up to 8B and out-of-domain tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning fine-tuning improves LLM reasoning by using reward signals on sampled rollouts, but when only a subset of rollouts has reward labels, the rest are typically unused. Semi-supervised learning aims to propagate labels from labeled to unlabeled data, but existing methods often ignore the relational structure among queries, reasoning processes, and answers. Graph neural networks, especially heterogeneous GNNs, are designed to model such relational data by learning embeddings that preserve heterogeneous structure and semantics.

## [Study questions whether more faithful XAI explanations improve human understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25251) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A user study (N=200) manipulated explanation correctness at four levels (100%, 85%, 70%, 55%) in a synthetic time series classification task and found that forward simulation accuracy dropped only at 70% and 55% relative to 100%, with no conclusive difference between 85% and 100%. This challenges the common assumption in XAI evaluation that higher functional correctness (faithfulness) always leads to better human understanding, suggesting that functional metrics need validation against human outcomes and that small correctness differences may not matter for users. Lower correctness reduced the number of participants who predicted accurately rather than uniformly lowering accuracy, and even with fully correct explanations only a subset of participants performed well above chance; some participants predicted correctly but described the wrong pattern, and self-reported ratings correlated with accuracy only when explanations were fully correct.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Explainable AI (XAI) methods are often evaluated using functional correctness metrics—also called faithfulness or fidelity—which estimate how closely an explanation reflects a model's actual reasoning. Feature attribution explanations assign importance scores to input features, and higher correctness is typically assumed to produce better human understanding, but this link had not been tested with controlled levels. Forward simulation, where users predict a model's decisions from its explanations, is a common proxy for understanding in XAI user studies.

## [Ordinal Credit Assignment: Multi-Step RL from Pairwise Comparisons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06375) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Ordinal Credit Assignment, a method that enables multi-step reinforcement learning using only pairwise trajectory comparisons, without any reward function or reward model. It proves that the expected update approximates policy gradient with win rates replacing reward-to-go, and shows robustness to label noise near ties or from position bias. This work provides a theoretical foundation for policy optimization from human preferences alone, which is directly relevant to RLHF and preference-based learning where scalar rewards are hard to specify. It could simplify reward modeling pipelines and enable more accurate learning from LLM judges, as experiments show comparisons are 7-10 points more accurate than pointwise scores. In deterministic environments, the update ranks actions by true value and the optimal policy is stable, but under stochastic outcomes it prefers actions that win more often rather than those with higher mean. A critic trained from the same comparisons removes state confound without changing the expected update, and in a pre-registered study over six environments, comparisons cost no detectable performance relative to scalar returns, though they do not close the gap to PPO with scalar rewards on HalfCheetah.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning typically relies on a scalar reward function to guide policy optimization, but designing such rewards is difficult for complex tasks. Preference-based RL, such as RLHF, instead learns from pairwise comparisons of outcomes, but often requires training a separate reward model. Credit assignment—determining which actions led to a reward—is a core challenge in multi-step tasks, and this paper addresses it directly using comparisons.

## [HilbNets: Geometric Deep Learning for Infinite-Dimensional Signals via Hilbert Bundles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06395) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces HilbNets, a convolutional learning framework for possibly infinite-dimensional signals on manifolds, using the connection Laplacian of a Hilbert bundle as the convolutional operator. It also provides a two-stage sampling procedure to make the convolution implementable, proving that the sheaf Laplacian converges to the connection Laplacian and that discretized networks are consistent and transferable. This work extends geometric deep learning to settings where signals at each point live in their own Hilbert space, generalizing classical Laplacian-based frameworks like Belkin & Niyogi's convergence result. It could impact applications involving time series, probability distributions, or operators defined on irregular domains. The two-stage sampling first induces a Hilbert Cellular Sheaf whose sheaf Laplacian converges in probability to the connection Laplacian as sampling density increases, and second discretizes the signals to yield implementable networks. The framework is validated on synthetic and real-world tasks, though practical impact remains niche.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Geometric deep learning aims to generalize deep learning to non-Euclidean domains such as graphs and manifolds. The connection Laplacian is a differential operator on tensor bundles of a manifold, and cellular sheaves provide a discrete framework for assigning data to cells with consistency relations. Hilbert bundles generalize vector bundles by allowing infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces as fibers, enabling the representation of signals like distributions or operators at each point.

## [vOPD stabilizes on-policy distillation with closed-form control variate baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07865) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes vOPD, a method that stabilizes on-policy distillation (OPD) for large language models by introducing a control variate baseline derived from a closed-form value function. This baseline is the per-token negative reverse KL divergence between student and teacher, computed directly from the existing forward pass without additional critic or inference overhead. OPD is a dominant post-training paradigm for LLMs, especially in reasoning domains, but its instability due to high gradient variance has hindered practical adoption. vOPD offers a principled and efficient stabilization that matches the performance of expensive full-vocabulary methods, potentially making OPD more accessible and reliable for researchers and practitioners. vOPD preserves the lightweight single-sample Monte Carlo estimator and subtracts the value function as a detached baseline to keep the gradient unbiased while reducing variance. A top-k approximation of the baseline further lowers cost without compromising performance, and experiments across mathematics, science, and instruction-following benchmarks show consistent improvements over base OPD.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own generated outputs while aligning with a teacher model's distribution, often using reverse KL divergence. However, the single-sample Monte Carlo estimator of the gradient has high variance, leading to unstable training. Control variates, such as value functions in reinforcement learning, are used to reduce variance without introducing bias.

## [Every Finite Group Admits a Just Finite Presentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10402) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Marc Lackenby proves that every finite group admits a 'just finite' presentation, meaning that removing any relation from the presentation yields an infinite group, thereby resolving Kourovka Notebook Problem 21.10 affirmatively. This resolves a long-standing open problem in combinatorial group theory, providing a clean affirmative answer that will be of high interest to researchers in group theory and related areas of pure mathematics. The result is a consequence of a stronger theorem: every finitely presented group with Property (T) admits a finite 'just-(T)' presentation, and the construction is explicit.

**Background**: A group presentation < X | R > describes a group by generators X and relations R; it is finite if both sets are finite. A 'just finite' presentation is one where deleting any single relation makes the group infinite. The Kourovka Notebook is a famous collection of open problems in group theory, and Problem 21.10 asked whether every finite group has such a presentation.

## [GraphIP-Bench: Unified Benchmark for GNN Model Extraction and Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12827) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GraphIP-Bench is a new unified benchmark that evaluates twelve model-extraction attacks and twelve ownership defenses on graph neural networks under a single black-box protocol, spanning ten graphs, three GNN backbones, and three tasks. It also introduces a joint attack-and-defense track that measures watermark verification on extracted surrogates, revealing that many watermarks lose most of their signal after extraction. Prior work on GNN extraction and defense used inconsistent datasets, threat models, and metrics, making results incomparable; this benchmark provides a shared experimental protocol that can guide both future research and practical deployment decisions for companies serving GNNs as cloud APIs. Its finding that most defenses fail to prevent extraction and that watermarks degrade on surrogates highlights a real gap in intellectual-property protection for graph models. The benchmark reports fidelity, task utility, ownership verification, and computational cost on shared splits, queries, and budgets, and finds that stealing a GNN is easy at medium query budgets while heterophilic graphs are systematically harder to steal and cross-architecture mismatch reduces but does not prevent extraction. The attacks and defenses are integrated into the PyGIP library with reproducible scripts and configurations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are increasingly deployed as cloud services, where an adversary can query the model and train a surrogate to mimic its behavior—a model-extraction attack. Ownership defenses include watermarking (embedding identifiable signals into the model), output perturbation, and query-pattern detection, but prior evaluations varied widely. Homophilic graphs connect nodes of the same class, while heterophilic graphs connect dissimilar nodes, and the latter are known to be harder for GNNs to learn from.

## [Stable Blanket Predictors Outperform Causal Parents in Prediction-Intervention Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16828) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces prediction-intervention games, a Stackelberg game where a leader chooses a predictor and a follower intervenes on covariates, and proves that predictors based on the stable blanket (an invariant subset) are at least as good as those based on causal parents for two common follower objectives. This bridges causal inference and game theory, offering theoretical guarantees for robust prediction in strategic settings where agents can manipulate inputs, with implications for causal machine learning and decision-making under strategic behavior. The authors upper bound the leader's post-intervention risk by a worst-case risk over allowed interventions, give sufficient conditions for stable-blanket predictors to be worst-case optimal, and show these conditions cannot be dropped; they also discuss practical strategies for known and unknown graphs and test on simulated and real-world data.

**Background**: A Stackelberg game is a strategic game where a leader moves first and a follower responds, as in economics. In causal inference, invariant prediction identifies causal predictors by exploiting stability across environments. The stable blanket is an invariant subset of covariates that generalizes the Markov blanket, ensuring stable prediction under interventions.

## [AvalancheBench: New Benchmark Tests Data Agents on End-to-End Semantic Query Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24183) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AvalancheBench is a new multimodal enterprise analytics benchmark that evaluates data agents on their ability to recover latent world specifications from 58 questions across text, images, and documents, exposing an 'avalanche effect' where errors propagate through dependent questions. The benchmark also introduces capability-adjusted token cost (CATC) and evaluates 5 model-harness configurations across 3 agent harnesses using Snowflake Cortex AISQL, with the best reaching a macro analytical fidelity of 0.82. Existing benchmarks evaluate agents and semantic query engines in isolation, so AvalancheBench fills a gap by testing end-to-end analytical workflows where agents must decide when and how to use semantic operators. Its findings—that requirement misinterpretation causes 51.5% of lost points and that 29% of lost points come from reusing another question's results—highlight critical failure modes for enterprise data agents and could guide the design of more robust agent systems. The benchmark's 3 validated tasks contain 58 questions provided as a multi-part analytical brief, and errors propagate through legitimate dependencies, with 45.1% of lost points from failing to recover facts requiring semantic computation and only 2.5% from incorrect operator outputs. Monetary cost and CATC produce different Pareto frontiers, distinguishing economically attractive configurations from technically resource-efficient ones.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Semantic query processing engines (SQPEs) extend classical relational databases with LLM-powered semantic operators—such as semantic filters, joins, and classification—to handle structured and unstructured data. Data agents are LLM-based systems that act as an interface for analytics, deciding which operations to apply. AvalancheBench builds on this by generating observed data from latent world specifications, meaning the underlying ground-truth facts and relationships that agents must infer, and evaluating how well agents recover that world through end-to-end analysis.

## [Mixture of Activations: Token-Adaptive FFN Layers Boost LLM Expressivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26647) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Mixture of Activations (MoA), a token-adaptive feedforward network (FFN) layer that mixes a dictionary of activation functions using lightweight input-dependent gates while sharing linear projections. It also introduces learnable activations (LA) as an input-independent counterpart, and theoretically proves strict finite-width expressive separations: LA strictly contains fixed-activation FFNs, and MoA strictly contains LA. FFN layers account for a large fraction of parameters and nonlinear expressivity in Transformer-based LLMs, yet most designs still use a single fixed activation function for all tokens. MoA's token-adaptive mixing could offer a simple and effective mechanism to improve FFN expressivity with minimal parameter and computational overhead, potentially impacting future LLM architecture design. The theoretical analysis establishes strict finite-width expressive separations among fixed-activation FFNs, LA, and MoA, with the additional expressivity of MoA arising from input-dependent nonlinear hybridization. Empirically, MoA was evaluated through extensive pre-training experiments on dense and MoE language models ranging from 0.12B to 2B parameters under different token budgets, optimizers, and learning rate schedules, consistently achieving lower terminal loss and more favorable scaling behavior than well-tuned baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Feedforward network (FFN) layers are a core component of Transformer-based large language models (LLMs), responsible for a large share of parameters and nonlinear transformation. Activation functions like ReLU, GELU, and gated variants such as SwiGLU introduce nonlinearity, but typically a single fixed activation is applied to all tokens. SwiGLU, for example, combines linear projections with the Swish function and a gating mechanism to enhance expressivity. The paper's theoretical framework builds on studies of neural network expressive power, particularly finite-width separations, to show that token-adaptive activation mixing is strictly more expressive than fixed or learnable activations.
