<a id="item-234"></a>
## [Recon: Reconstruction-Guided Reasoning Synthesis for User Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Recon introduces a method that scores synthesized reasoning traces by how well they allow a reconstruction model to predict a user's action from context, rather than conditioning the trace on the action itself. Across four domains, Recon achieves a 54.7% win rate over the Backward Synthesis baseline, and training a reasoning synthesis model with rewards derived from Recon yields up to a 70.0% win rate over baselines. This work addresses a key limitation of action-conditioned reasoning traces, which are post-hoc rationalizations that may not reflect a user's true decision process. By showing that reconstruction-based scoring produces more causally faithful and transferable reasoning, it offers a valuable technical contribution to language model-based user simulation for behavioral science, human-AI collaboration, and market research. Recon uses a reconstruction model to predict the action given context and candidate reasoning, with reconstruction fidelity determining reasoning quality; the synthesized reasoning also transfers across models and improves user modeling beyond the reconstruction model itself. The approach is validated across four domains and outperforms the standard post-hoc rationalization baseline, Backward Synthesis.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: User modeling with language models aims to simulate an individual's behavior from past context-action pairs, such as conversation turns. Recent approaches augment these corpora with synthesized reasoning traces, but typically generate them by conditioning on both context and action, which produces post-hoc rationalizations that justify the action rather than reveal the underlying decision process. Post-hoc rationalization is a known failure mode where a model commits to an answer first and constructs a narrative afterward, making the reasoning fragile and less useful for faithful simulation.

## [MusTBench and MusT: New Benchmark and Training Recipe for Temporal Grounding in Music LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29300) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MusTBench, a benchmark with stem-wise data and music-expert validation that evaluates temporal grounding in large audio-language models (LALMs) across five temporally grounded question-answering tasks. They also proposed MusT, a four-stage training recipe covering music encoder adaptation, LLM adaptation, supervised fine-tuning, and RL-based optimization, which consistently improves temporal grounding across different LALM backbones and scales. This work addresses an underexplored but critical gap: whether LALMs actually ground their answers in the correct temporal regions of music, where key events like instrument entries and rhythmic transitions are localized. By providing both a validated benchmark and a reusable training recipe, it could spur more reliable temporally grounded music understanding research and applications. MusTBench uses stem-wise data with expert validation and five temporally grounded QA tasks, and experiments show existing LALMs struggle with precise temporal grounding. MusT's four-stage recipe spans music encoder adaptation, LLM adaptation, LLM supervised fine-tuning, and RL-based optimization, improving performance across backbones and scales.

**Background**: Large audio-language models (LALMs) extend multimodal LLMs to audio, enabling them to answer questions about music and other sounds. Temporal grounding means pinpointing exactly when an event occurs in the audio, a capability that recent work such as SpotSound and TAG-Bench has shown LALMs often lack. Music is a particularly demanding domain because important information appears as short, localized events like instrument entries and rhythmic transitions. Reinforcement learning has proven effective for aligning LLMs with desired behaviors, and MusT applies it as the final stage of its training pipeline.

## [InDex Adapts VLA Models from Parallel Grippers to Dexterous Hands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12109) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce InDex, an intent-conditioned fine-tuning framework that adapts pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models from parallel grippers to multi-finger dexterous manipulation. Using a normalized grasp-intent signal and a diffusion hand decoder, InDex with the π0.5 backbone achieves 85.8% mean success across four simulated tasks, a 35.5 percentage point improvement over the native policy. This work addresses a real gap in VLA models, whose parallel-gripper interfaces leave multi-finger contact coordination implicit, and shows that arm-hand coordination is an actionable bottleneck. The approach generalizes across three VLA backbones and real-world policies, potentially accelerating the adoption of dexterous hands in embodied AI. InDex uses a two-stage training scheme: Stage 1 learns synchronized end-effector-intent chunks, and Stage 2 conditions a diffusion hand decoder on these predictions, visual-language context, and proprioception. It reaches 69.0% success on held-out objects with similar geometry without fine-tuning or recalibration, and improves real-world policies from 71.0% to 83.0% mean success.

Diffusion models have emerged as effective generative models for robot action policies, including hand motion generation.

## [Statistical Framework Audits Privacy in Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16952) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a customizable empirical auditing framework that uses statistical hypothesis testing to detect and explain privacy disclosures in synthetic data, distinguishing true disclosures from phantom ones and checking consistency with privacy baselines like differential privacy. This framework provides a model-agnostic, computationally efficient way to audit synthetic data privacy without requiring model access, canary insertion, or reference model training, making it practical for real-world deployment and potentially tightening privacy leakage bounds compared to prior methods. The approach partitions input data into training and holdout sets and applies rigorous statistical hypothesis testing to determine if observed disclosures are consistent with strict privacy baselines such as zero-learning or specific Differential Privacy bounds; it functions as a membership inference attack and provides empirical lower bounds on privacy leakage that are tighter than prior data-based auditing methods, requiring orders of magnitude fewer computational resources than shadow-model or canary-based alternatives.

**Background**: Synthetic data is increasingly used as a privacy-preserving alternative to sensitive real-world datasets, but generative models can memorize and regurgitate private information. Differential privacy is a mathematical framework that provides provable privacy guarantees by injecting calibrated noise. Auditing synthetic data for privacy leakage typically involves membership inference attacks or canary insertion, which can be computationally expensive or require model access.

Researchers introduced PatientsWithPersonality (PWP), a patient simulation framework that uses the six-dimensional HEXACO personality model to parametrize a latent patient state, enabling fine-grained control over conversational style, cooperativeness, and information disclosure. In the first direct comparison of patient simulators under a shared protocol, clinicians judged PWP conversations as realistic at least as often as real recorded physician-patient encounters and far more often than prior simulators. Realistic and steerable patient simulation is essential for developing and benchmarking clinical LLMs at scale without costly human studies, and PWP's ability to reproduce real patients' disclosure pacing and behavioral range means downstream evaluations of LLMs interviewing patients are more faithful to real-world performance. PWP conditions responses on a latent patient state and HEXACO personality traits, which clinicians and an autorater could recover from conversations, and it almost never produced responses flagged as 'too informative'. In a downstream benchmark, LLMs interviewing PWP patients performed closest to their performance on real encounters, whereas prior simulators made the task easier.

## [Variable Bound Tightening Speeds Nash Equilibrium Computation in Multiplayer Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25997) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper derives finite bounds on slack and multiplier variables in a nonlinear complementarity formulation for exact Nash equilibrium computation in multiplayer imperfect-information games. On full three-player Kuhn poker, all eight bounded configurations return numerically verified equilibria in under 17 seconds, while the unbounded formulation finds no feasible solution within 300 seconds. This work enables exact Nash equilibrium computation for larger multiplayer imperfect-information games that previously could not be solved within practical time limits, addressing a key limitation of scalable algorithms like counterfactual regret minimization that lack convergence guarantees in multiplayer settings. The bounds strengthen convex relaxations within spatial branch-and-bound, but the finest bounds do not consistently yield the fastest computation, and the approach still relies on Gurobi's nonconvex quadratic solver with auxiliary and binary variables introduced during presolve.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Nash equilibrium is a solution concept in game theory where no player can benefit by unilaterally changing strategy. Imperfect-information games model situations where players have private information, and the sequence-form representation reduces the strategy space by considering sequences of actions. Spatial branch-and-bound is a global optimization method that partitions the feasible region and uses convex relaxations to find bounds, while quadratically constrained programs involve quadratic functions in both objective and constraints.

## [SMT Jailbreak Exploits Function-Calling LLMs via Fake Moderation Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.00481) introduces SMT (Simulated Moderation Traces), a black-box jailbreak framework that constructs multi-turn trajectories simulating a legitimate moderation-auditing workflow to elicit harmful outputs from function-calling LLMs. Evaluations on commercial LLMs from five providers across two safety benchmarks show SMT achieves the highest average attack success rate and HarmScore with near-minimal queries, outperforming existing baselines. This work reveals a structural vulnerability in stateful, function-calling LLM environments that prompt-level defenses overlook, showing that adversarial intent can be distributed across multi-turn execution paths. It underscores the urgent need for context-aware validation across schemas, arguments, tool outputs, and accumulated conversation state, affecting developers deploying tool-enabled LLM systems. SMT is a black-box attack that fabricates a moderation frame using red-team testing as a pretext, then treats safety refusals as execution failures in validation feedback to gradually weaken safety constraints. The code is available at https://github.com/liujlong27/SMT, and the attack requires only a near-minimal number of queries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Function calling allows LLMs to invoke external tools with structured arguments, enabling complex workflows but interleaving trusted control logic with untrusted data in a shared context. Prior jailbreak research focused on prompt-level attacks, but this paper shows that stateful, multi-turn interactions expand the attack surface. SMT simulates a moderation audit by exposing two function schemas: a case generator that requests paired safe/unsafe samples and a case validator with criteria, thereby bypassing prompt-level sanitization.

A new arXiv paper (2607.05017v2) develops and validates a hyperparameter transfer parameterization for graph neural networks trained with SGD, Adam, and AdamW, showing stable feature updates and learning rate transfer across width and depth. The work identifies graph-dependent first-layer correction factors for SGD and explores how message passing normalization affects transfer behavior for Adam. This extends hyperparameter transfer, previously well-studied for dense networks in language and vision, to GNNs, potentially enabling practitioners to tune small proxy GNNs and reliably transfer hyperparameters to large-scale graph models. It provides a practical recipe for scaling GNNs across diverse learning tasks and training scenarios. For SGD, graph-dependent first-layer correction factors are shown to accelerate early training on graphs with sparse bag-of-words inputs. For Adam, different message passing normalizations affect early- and late-training transfer, motivating an associated hyperparameter; for AdamW, the parameterization allows joint transfer of weight decay and learning rate.

GaP introduces a multi-agent coding harness that generates directed computation graphs from a 51-skill Modular Open Robot Skill Library (MORSL) to automate variational tasks. It creates an internal simulation environment to rehearse task instances with different graphs in parallel, iteratively refining graph structure and parameters to improve success rates and throughput. GaP is evaluated on 8 new open variational automation benchmarks (4 in simulation, 4 in real-world) and significantly outperforms baselines. ## [Token Time Continuous Diffusion improves language modeling at high speedups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14106) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces token time continuous diffusion (TTCD), a continuous-space diffusion language model that assigns per-token times so some tokens denoise faster than others, and reports that a 160M-parameter model trained on OpenWebText and self-distilled matches or beats similar discrete models at high speedups. This work targets a key weakness of discrete diffusion language models—accuracy loss from parallel token sampling at high speedups—and could make diffusion-based text generation more competitive with autoregressive models for fast conditional generation tasks such as Sudoku solving. TTCD deterministically maps Gaussian noise to a final token canvas without further sampling, and its per-token times allow more confident tokens to proceed faster and enable differentiated inter-token influences during refinement; gains are reported for unconditional and conditional generation and Sudoku solving, though the evaluation uses a relatively small 160M model on OpenWebText.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion models generate data by gradually denoising random noise, and language versions typically work either in discrete space by masking tokens or in continuous space by embedding tokens as vectors. Discrete diffusion models often sample multiple tokens in parallel to speed up generation, which can hurt accuracy at high speedups. Continuous diffusion avoids some of this by modeling tokens in a continuous embedding space, and self-distillation trains a faster student model using the original model as a teacher.

## [Evaluation Agent Middleware Detects Misinformation and Poisoning in RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21095) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2608.21095v2) proposes an Evaluation Agent middleware for RAG systems that combines NLI-based factual verification, a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and a Trust Index (T = 0.4F + 0.35C + 0.25(1-P)) with a non-linear dampener. On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, it reports 91% accuracy and 100% precision, with 100% recall on instruction injection, while noting that in-place edits such as entity swaps remain hard to detect. RAG systems typically trust whatever they retrieve, creating a security-reliability gap where high semantic relevance does not guarantee factual truth, and adversaries can exploit this through knowledge poisoning. This work offers a concrete, deployable middleware design with strong reported results, addressing a real and underexplored security-reliability gap that affects any organization deploying RAG-based generative AI. The Trust Index remains discriminative across three LLMs with ROC-AUC of 0.73 to 0.81, and generation style matters more than model size, with per-LLM threshold calibration restoring baseline competitive accuracy. A weaker FEVER result shows cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration, and in a software-engineering use case over OWASP Top 10 and CWE guidance, the agent blocks instruction injection of unsafe advice with F1 92%, though contradiction and subtle semantic weakening remain hard; the agent measures detection of poisoned context before generation, not whether the LLM adopts the injected misinformation.

## [LongCounsel-8: A New Benchmark for Tracking Depression Across Counseling Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03507) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced LongCounsel-8, a benchmark of 7,749 synthetic five-session counseling trajectories generated by three LLMs, designed to evaluate whether models can track depression changes over time rather than scoring each session independently. The benchmark uses a systematic pipeline combining real client profiles, real longitudinal depression scores, and real dialogue turn lengths and forms, with each session labeled to express a preset PHQ-8 score. Existing dialogue benchmarks score each session in isolation, so a method can be accurate per session yet fail to follow changes between sessions—exactly what clinical evidence says matters most for treatment monitoring. LongCounsel-8 fills this gap and shows that all five existing methods tested fall short, which could reshape how LLM-based mental health assessment is evaluated. Validation indicates the labels are self-consistent and the dialogues are close to real counseling, but the data is synthetic and generated by LLMs. The trend-based evaluation protocol measures between-session changes, crossings of the screening cutoff, trajectory types, and symptom-level changes; experiments show none of the five methods estimates changes better than a no-change baseline, at most 40% of cutoff crossings are detected, and adding earlier sessions lowers change error but causes more missed improvements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: The PHQ-8 is a standard eight-symptom depression questionnaire derived from the nine-item Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ-9), commonly used in clinical screening and research to measure depression severity. In counseling, psychologists emphasize tracking symptom change across sessions rather than relying on any single score, but longitudinal counseling data with standardized session-level depression labels is scarce. LongCounsel-8 addresses this scarcity by synthesizing multi-session trajectories grounded in real profiles, scores, and dialogue form, enabling controlled evaluation of longitudinal tracking.

## [PolicyMem compiles natural-language policies into low-rank subspaces for LLM governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13734) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PolicyMem introduces a geometric policy memory that compiles natural-language policies into learnable low-rank subspaces, enabling explicit and reusable policy representations across assessment, rewriting, and verification stages. The policy assessor projects query-response representations onto these subspaces to produce evidence for safety judgments and policy attribution, while the same memory supervises a response rewriter without external teacher calls. This approach bridges the gap between implicit learned guards and manually engineered programmable guardrails, offering a more explicit and reusable policy representation for LLM safety. It could improve closed-loop governance by enabling consistent policy application across detection, correction, and verification, impacting AI safety researchers and practitioners building guardrails. PolicyMem achieves the best average unsafe-behavior detection performance among representative baselines across five widely used benchmarks, while also supporting policy attribution, rewriting, and post-intervention assessment through the shared memory. The approach relies on low-rank subspaces to encode policies, which may impose constraints on expressiveness and scalability compared to full-rank representations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: LLM guardrails are safety layers that monitor and control model inputs and outputs, often implemented as learned classifiers or programmable rule-based systems. Learned guards are semantically strong but keep policy knowledge implicit in parameters, while programmable guardrails like NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails make policies explicit through prompts and workflows but require manual engineering. Low-rank subspaces are a technique that captures essential directions in high-dimensional data, commonly used for efficient adaptation and representation learning.

<a id="item-248"></a>
## [Hutter et al. Propose Relativized Solomonoff Induction to Explain AI Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34458) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper by Hutter and colleagues argues that classical statistical theory cannot explain the success of general-purpose AI models because it relies on unjustified handcrafted inductive biases. The authors propose relativizing Solomonoff induction to an 'information vantage point,' biasing toward short programs that have access to all preexisting information, thereby reframing the inductive bias as accessibility relative to our vantage point rather than an absolute notion of simplicity. This work offers a formal answer to the meta No Free Lunch problem, suggesting that algorithmic information theory should play a fundamental role in explaining the generalization behavior of modern and future AI systems. It provides a theoretical optimum for inference in the limit of infinite compute and may guide how researchers think about the inductive biases of frontier models. The relativized Solomonoff induction is incomputable and thus not a practical algorithm, but it serves as a formal optimum for inference given infinite compute, and the authors note evidence that frontier AI systems roughly approximate it. An algorithm can only outpredict the relativized SI to the extent that its code contains additional information about the data, and no algorithm can generate such information.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Solomonoff induction is a theory of inductive inference that assigns higher prior probability to shorter programs, formalizing Occam's razor, and it is known to be incomputable but approximable. The No Free Lunch (NFL) theorems state that averaged over all possible problems, all optimization algorithms perform equally well, implying that any learner that beats chance on some environments must underperform on others. Inductive bias refers to the set of assumptions a learning algorithm uses to predict outputs for unseen inputs, and it is essential for generalization beyond training data.

## [SimForcing Distills Simulation Motion Priors into Real Robot World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06598) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SimForcing is a simulation-guided framework that transfers motion knowledge from a simulation teacher to a real-domain robot world model via latent-motion distillation and multi-block simulation conditioning with condition dropout. On the Bridge dataset, it achieves the best PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, and FVD among compared methods without external embodied pretraining, and also improves LIBERO success when used to initialize a vision-language-action model. This addresses a core sim-to-real challenge: simulation provides structured motion supervision but its appearance gap hinders direct transfer, and inaccurate simulation predictions can misguide real-video generation. By distilling motion priors rather than pixels, the method could make action-conditioned world models more accurate and useful for downstream policy learning in robotics. The approach aligns temporal changes in latent space to internalize motion priors while mitigating appearance differences, and uses a simulation-conditioning classifier-free guidance scheme to balance internalized knowledge with simulation-guided predictions. The jointly trained student generates both simulation conditions and real-domain videos, so no additional world model is needed at inference; evaluation on InternData-A1 further supports cross-dataset applicability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Action-conditioned robot world models predict how a scene evolves in response to robot actions, typically as video, and are used for planning and policy learning. Sim-to-real transfer aims to leverage simulated data for real robots, but appearance differences between simulation and reality create a gap. Knowledge distillation trains a student model to inherit capabilities from a teacher model, and here the teacher is a simulation-based model whose motion knowledge is transferred to a real-domain world model.

## [Fractional Power-of-Two Quantization Enables Multiplier-Free 4-bit LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21257) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Fractional Power-of-Two (FPoT) quantization, which uses a base-√2 (half-octave) weight grid instead of the conventional base-2 grid to achieve practical 4-bit weight quantization while preserving a fully integer shift-add datapath. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weight and activation quantization, FPoT improves zero-shot commonsense reasoning by 8.72% relative to the PoT baseline, with only 0.4 percentage-point degradation compared to the uniform integer baseline. This work addresses the accuracy degradation of power-of-two quantization at low bit-widths, enabling multiplier-free inference that could significantly reduce compute and energy costs in LLM deployment. The framework-agnostic approach can be integrated with state-of-the-art post-training quantization methods, making it broadly applicable to efficient LLM hardware acceleration. FPoT combines a base-√2 weight grid with uniform integer activations, a calibration-time grid-alignment refinement absorbed into static per-channel scales, and a lightweight 1-adder √2 approximation whose error is absorbed during weight calibration. The resulting processing element uses significantly fewer gates than a 4-bit integer multiplier, and results hold across seven models and three bit-widths.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models rely heavily on multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations in linear projections, which dominate compute and energy costs during inference. Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces these costs by mapping weights and activations to low-bit integers, and power-of-two (PoT) weight quantization further promises multiplier-free inference using shift-add logic. However, conventional PoT uses a coarse base-2 grid that causes significant accuracy loss at low bit-widths, motivating finer-grained alternatives like the base-√2 grid proposed here.

## [X-Fly: Staged Training Boosts Full-Chunk Aerial VLN Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21450) ⭐️ 7.0/10

X-Fly introduces a staged training framework that combines Recovery DAgger and Flow-GRPO to address the high rate of no-stop failures (47.78%) in full-chunk aerial vision-language navigation. It achieves 74.18% success rate on OpenFly test-seen and 51.73% on test-unseen, compared to 16.45% and 18.43% for full-chunk behavior cloning, while reducing per-waypoint inference from 195 ms to 42 ms. This work tackles a critical failure mode in full-chunk aerial VLN, where executing entire action chunks before replanning improves efficiency but causes the policy to encounter unfamiliar states. By enabling reliable stopping and recovery, X-Fly significantly improves success rates, making aerial VLN more practical for real-world deployment where inference cost and safety are paramount. Recovery DAgger labels learner-visited decision states with smooth chunks that reconnect to future expert segments and with STOP at the first in-region state, while Flow-GRPO jointly refines the chunked flow controller and a Bernoulli STOP policy from closed-loop outcomes. The method uses eight controls per decision and is evaluated on the OpenFly benchmark, showing substantial gains over baselines like DreamFly (32.04% and 29.46% success rates).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) aims to enable UAVs to follow natural language instructions using visual perception, typically built on large vision-language models that are expensive to query. Full-chunk execution amortizes inference cost by running a complete predicted action chunk before replanning, but it shifts the policy to states not covered by expert demonstrations, leading to failures such as not stopping at the goal. DAgger is an imitation learning algorithm that iteratively collects data from the learner's state distribution and labels it with expert actions, while Flow-GRPO integrates online reinforcement learning into flow matching models via group-relative policy optimization.

## [RefCraft: New Benchmark Tests AI Agents on Visual-Reference Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RefCraft, a benchmark of 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret these visual references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs, and the strongest of nine frontier (M)LLMs scored only 52.12 out of 100. Existing web-development benchmarks largely ignore the common real-world workflow of building from visual examples, so RefCraft fills a genuine evaluation gap for visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation. Its low scores show that even top multimodal models struggle with this practical productivity task, pointing to a clear direction for future agent research. RefCraft evaluates open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints plus complementary programmatic and VLM judges, and tasks require agents to handle one to five reference screenshots rather than a single target. The benchmark is a cross-listed replacement (v2) on arXiv and focuses on selective interpretation of references rather than simple reconstruction.

**Background**: Agentic generation refers to AI systems that plan, use tools, inspect intermediate outputs, and iteratively revise their work rather than producing a single one-shot result. In web development, prior benchmarks typically ask models to reconstruct a visual target or generate a page from text alone, but real developers often work from screenshots of existing sites as references. RefCraft targets this gap by requiring multimodal agents to decide which parts of multiple references to borrow and to refine the page through visual inspection.

<a id="item-253"></a>
## [ASTEROID: Asymmetric On-Policy Distillation Learns In-Context Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30105) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ASTEROID is a student-teacher supervised learning framework that trains a history-conditioned student to perform test-time exploration by imitating a privileged expert on the student's own on-policy contexts. Across eleven environments, it achieves roughly 2x higher returns under fixed exploration budgets, uses about 100x fewer interactions than RL baselines, and improves real-world robot manipulation success by +40% over standard distillation. This work shows that efficient exploration can be learned through supervised distillation rather than sample-expensive online reinforcement learning, which could make partially observable sequential decision-making far more practical. It is relevant to reinforcement learning and imitation learning researchers, as well as roboticists who need agents to gather information under hidden state. The student first collects on-policy context under partial observability and then imitates a privileged expert conditioned on that context, learning to search while uncertain and exploit once the hidden state is revealed, akin to Bayesian posterior sampling. The approach avoids the impractical training-data costs of RL and transfers from simulation to vision-denied real-world robot manipulation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: In partially observable sequential decision-making, agents must explore to infer hidden task-relevant information before acting effectively. Learning such information-gathering behavior from scratch via online reinforcement learning is sample-inefficient, while offline imitation averages expert actions across hidden states and fails to learn exploration. Privileged experts, which have access to the hidden state, never need to explore, so distilling them naively does not teach exploration.

## [SPARTA: Sparse Ternary Accelerator for Long-Context Edge LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34824) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SPARTA introduces a sparse ternary LLM accelerator that unifies ternary SpMM/SpMV and bit-serial attention on shared processing elements, and adds an adaptive two-stage attention engine combining page-level Top-k selection with token-level Top-p pruning. It reduces KV-cache traffic by 4.1–4.8x over full attention and 1.3–1.5x over a state-of-the-art sparse-attention scheme, achieving 3.9x higher generation throughput and 8.3x higher energy efficiency than NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. Long-context inference on edge devices is increasingly bottlenecked by KV-cache traffic rather than weight storage, so co-designing sparsity, attention, and datapath reuse directly targets a real deployment constraint. The reported gains over Jetson AGX Orin and desktop CPUs suggest ternary LLMs could become practical for memory- and bandwidth-limited edge hardware. The design exploits heterogeneous sparsity across compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode via a phase-aware sparse dataflow, and uses bit-serial attention to capture zero-bit sparsity. The paper notes task-dependent quality loss from the adaptive attention pruning, and reports 1.6–12.0x higher throughput, 2.0–4.2x higher energy efficiency, and 1.3–51.4x higher area efficiency than FIGLUT, TeLLMe, and MCBP.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Ternary (1.58-bit) LLMs restrict weights to −1, 0, and +1, sharply cutting model storage and enabling multiplication-free computation, as popularized by Microsoft's BitNet b1.58. However, long-context inference still requires a KV cache whose memory footprint grows linearly with context length, making cache traffic a first-order bottleneck. Sparse matrix-vector and matrix-matrix multiplication (SpMV/SpMM) accelerators exploit zero weights, but prior designs typically use separate datapaths for linear layers and attention, adding hardware and execution overhead.

## [LLM Driver Agents Show Mandatory Algorithm Disclosure Reshapes Ride-Hailing Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.41132v2) uses self-interested LLM driver agents to evaluate how disclosing rebalancing policy rules, as required by the EU Platform Work Directive, affects driver behavior under reactive (R) versus anticipatory (A) policies. Testing Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev in a three-zone model and Manhattan, it finds that with disclosed rules, drivers follow platform relocation offers much more closely—in Manhattan, the share of declined offers falls from about 80% to 25%—but the policy consequences diverge sharply. This is the first study to use LLM agents to evaluate ride-hailing policies under the EU's mandatory algorithmic transparency requirement, showing that policies that perform equally well under full compliance can produce opposite outcomes once rules are disclosed. The findings are relevant to platforms, regulators, and researchers preparing for the December 2026 transposition deadline, as they highlight how disclosure can alter platform profits, driver income, and service quality in non-obvious ways. Under disclosure, reactive policy (R) loses 3.7% of platform profit in Manhattan because the platform now pays for relocations drivers previously made for free, while anticipatory policy (A) profit is unchanged, leaving the two policies 6.1% apart despite tying under full compliance. In the small model, disclosure raises driver income under both policies and LLMs and boosts the service rate by 10–18 percentage points; in Manhattan, self-interested drivers make A a win-win-win, raising driver income, platform profit, and service rate by 10–12% each.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: The EU Platform Work Directive, adopted in October 2024 with a transposition deadline of December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to explain in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how worker behavior affects them. Rebalancing policies are algorithms that reposition idle drivers to match anticipated demand; reactive policies respond to recently lost requests, while anticipatory policies plan moves based on demand forecasts. LLM agents are AI models that can read and reason about natural-language rules, making them suitable for simulating how drivers might respond when algorithmic policies are disclosed.

## [Concept audits of melanoma classifiers tested with diffusion counterfactuals and matched controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02119) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper builds a counterfactual audit using a diffusion model conditioned on seven 7-point checklist concepts and tests it with matched positive controls and null classifiers. It finds that while the audit recovers targets for some concepts, it also produces false positives in null classifiers, highlighting the need for standardized effect margins. This work addresses a critical gap in evaluating concept-based explanations for medical AI, showing that without proper controls, audits can be misleading. It provides a rigorous methodology that could become a standard for validating interpretability methods in high-stakes domains like melanoma diagnosis. Colour-calibrated start noise reduced colour outliers from 78.5–84.8% to 6.6–13.3%, and four of seven knobs were target-dominant but also changed other concepts. The pigmentation sweep raised the melanoma logit in 15 of 15 ISIC 2020 classifiers (+3.12 on average) and 6 of 6 HAM10000 classifiers (+4.01), about four times the null response, but the regression effect did not transfer.

Counterfactual explanations generated by diffusion models allow testing how changes in concepts affect model outputs, while matched positive controls and null classifiers help detect false positives in audit methods. Concept activation vectors (TCAV) are a common technique to quantify concept importance in neural networks.

## [CUSD: Data-Free Model Merging via Common-Unique Subspace Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04108) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Common-Unique Subspace Decomposition (CUSD), a data-free pre-merging method that splits task vectors into a low-dispersion common component shared across tasks and a high-dispersion task-specific residual. Experiments across 11 base models, four model families, and four arithmetic merging operators show CUSD improves 42 of 44 model-operator combinations, with up to 22.5% gains over baselines. Model merging lets practitioners combine fine-tuned models without retraining, but existing arithmetic methods apply a single element-wise rule to all directions, ignoring differences in mergeability. CUSD's data-free separation of common and unique directions offers a plug-in improvement for popular operators like Task Arithmetic, TIES, and DARE, potentially making merged models more robust across diverse architectures. The method identifies a consistent two-regime structure in a shared directional basis: a common bulk with low dispersion and a task-dominated tail with high dispersion that carries disproportionate energy. CUSD merges only the common components with a downstream arithmetic operator while retaining unique residuals separately, and the code is publicly available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models into one without additional training, often using task vectors—the difference between fine-tuned and base model weights. Arithmetic methods like Task Arithmetic, TIES, and DARE apply simple element-wise operations to these vectors, but they treat all parameter directions uniformly. Prior work such as Task Singular Vectors (TSV) has explored subspace decomposition to reduce task interference, and CUSD extends this line by separating common and unique directions before merging.

<a id="item-258"></a>
## [Symmetric Recovery Needed for FP4 RL on Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04376) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper finds that dense-model FP4 RL recovery recipes fail for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs because they correct only the rollout side, causing training-inference policy misalignment. It proposes MoEResQ, a symmetric recovery recipe that applies corrections on both training and inference sides, restoring alignment and closing the accuracy gap to full precision. This work reveals a fundamental issue in low-precision RL post-training for MoE models, showing that naive adaptation of dense-model techniques is insufficient. It provides a concrete principle—symmetric recovery—that could enable efficient FP4 RL training for large MoE models, reducing memory and compute costs while maintaining accuracy. The analysis identifies the router's discrete expert-selection decisions as the root cause: asymmetric recovery (correcting only the rollout side) sharply deteriorates training-inference alignment, measured by Pearson correlation between training and inference probabilities and router selection matching rates. MoEResQ enforces symmetry between training- and inference-side corrections, substantially closing the accuracy gap to full precision.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: FP4 (4-bit floating point) quantization is an aggressive low-precision format that can reduce memory and compute costs for training and inference, but it introduces significant quantization errors. Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training for LLMs, such as PPO, relies on aligning the policy used for generating rollouts (inference) with the policy being trained, and mismatches can cause instability. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models route each token to a subset of experts via a router, which makes discrete decisions, adding complexity to maintaining alignment under quantization.

<a id="item-259"></a>
## [FloMo Uses 3D Scene Flow as Intermediate for Robot Learning from Human Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05062) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FloMo is a 5B video-generation model fine-tuned to predict 3D scene flow and action tokens, rendering scene flow as RGB and encoding it into the same latent space as video so a pretrained backbone can adapt efficiently. Co-trained on action-free human video and action-labeled robot teleoperation data, it generalizes to unseen objects, motion primitives, and tasks. This work provides empirical evidence that dense 3D motion, rather than full video, is a more sample-efficient and generalizable intermediate for transferring internet-scale video pretraining to robot policies. It suggests that predicting video may waste model capacity, which could reshape how future world-action models are designed for imitation learning. Replacing motion prediction with a video prediction objective degrades out-of-distribution generalization, and adding a video prediction target alongside motion lowers policy success rather than improving it. When motion, video, and action are predicted jointly, action tokens attend far more to motion than to video, indicating that video prediction is largely redundant for action prediction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: 3D scene flow is a dense 3D motion vector field describing how every point in a scene moves between consecutive frames, and it can be seen as the 3D counterpart of optical flow. Recent world-action models leverage video generation models to couple future visual prediction with action generation for robot learning. FloMo builds on this line of work by arguing that motion, not pixels, is the medium through which video pretraining best transfers to actions.

<a id="item-260"></a>
## [RoboTryOn turns human videos into robot demos via virtual try-on](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05163) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RoboTryOn proposes treating human-to-robot translation as a virtual try-on problem: it converts an egocentric human video into a video of a target robot specified by a reference image, while preserving the demonstrated motion, objects, and background. The method uses autoregressive keyframe translation and motion conditioned on the human hand skeleton, plus a depth-aware inverse dynamics model (IDM) to infer action labels for policy learning. This approach addresses the bottleneck of expensive teleoperated data for adapting robot foundation models, showing that policies trained on synthetic data can improve success on novel tasks from 2% to 60% without any real robot demonstrations. It could significantly lower the cost of collecting demonstration data for high-DoF humanoid robots. Experiments across multiple humanoid embodiments show that synthetic data matches the performance of policies trained solely on teleoperated demonstrations at approximately one-third of the collection cost. The approach relies on keyframe-guided video synthesis and a depth-aware IDM, though results are from an arXiv preprint and have not yet been validated in the excerpt.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Robot foundation models are large pre-trained models that can control robots, but adapting them to new tasks typically requires teleoperated demonstrations where a human operates the robot to collect data. Egocentric human videos offer a scalable alternative, but directly overlaying rendered robots or retargeting human poses leaves visual or action gaps for high-DoF robots. Virtual try-on is a video synthesis technique that swaps a garment on a person while preserving identity and motion, and RoboTryOn adapts this idea to swap the human for a robot.

## [Closed-Form Feasibility Condition for Confidence-Gated Tiered LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05394) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper derives a closed-form condition for when no confidence threshold makes a tiered LLM cascade worthwhile compared to sending every query to the backstop model. It identifies a signal-free floor and a perfect-signal ceiling on signal quality, and shows that the condition inverts along the cost ratio κ: when local compute is billed, cost binds, but when it is sunk (e.g., on decentralized hardware), feasibility becomes unconditional. This work provides a theoretical foundation for designing efficient LLM serving systems that distribute computation across devices, which is increasingly important for cost, latency, and privacy. It offers actionable design requirements and highlights when confidence-gated cascades are beneficial, potentially guiding both system architects and future research. The condition is derived by modeling routing as a binary test and separating local and escalation costs via an architecture cost ratio κ. The authors note that existing trained-in primitives satisfy the three derived design requirements only in isolation, and they propose a co-designed architecture and protocol as future work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Tiered inference distributes LLM computation across a hierarchy of devices, from on-device and edge hardware to the datacenter, to achieve cheaper, lower-latency, and more private serving. Such systems typically use frozen model checkpoints and rely on a confidence signal from a forward-deployed model to decide whether to resolve a query locally or escalate it to a larger backstop model. This paper provides a theoretical analysis of when such confidence-gated cascades are feasible compared to always using the backstop.

## [Prefix-Aware Non-Autoregressive Model Speeds Up Whole-Page Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05542) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Multi-Item Scoring (MIS), a prefix-aware non-autoregressive framework for whole-page ranking that incorporates sequential context into efficient multi-item scoring. Experiments show MIS achieves 4.07x and 3.54x faster CPU decoding than an autoregressive baseline on KuaiRand-1K and RL4RS, and in a large-scale social-video platform test, it improves the realization metric by 0.104% and watch time by 0.058% while reducing ranking-service CPU consumption by 9.73%. Whole-page ranking is critical in industrial recommendation and advertising systems, where balancing quality and inference efficiency is a persistent challenge. MIS narrows the quality gap between non-autoregressive and autoregressive methods while retaining the efficiency needed for large-scale deployment, potentially enabling more sophisticated page-level optimization in real-time systems. MIS integrates global candidate interactions, localized prefix conditioning, and position-aware scoring within a shared representation architecture, computing reusable representations once per request and performing lightweight prefix-conditioned scoring during constrained beam search. The gains are modest (0.104% realization, 0.058% watch time) but come with a 9.73% CPU reduction, indicating a favorable trade-off for industrial settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Whole-page ranking jointly optimizes ads and organic content under layout and business constraints. Non-autoregressive (NAR) methods score items independently for efficiency but miss order-dependent interactions, while autoregressive (AR) methods capture sequential dependencies at the cost of heavy inference. MIS aims to bridge this gap by adding prefix-aware sequential context to NAR-style scoring.

<a id="item-271"></a>
## [Study finds transparency gaps in generative AI across stakeholders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05549) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.05549) presents a global survey of 400 end-users, AI builders, and policymakers, plus a focus group with end-users, examining how each group understands and experiences transparency in generative AI. The study finds that while all groups share similar definitions of transparency, end-users perceive much larger gaps between the importance and availability of transparency features than builders or policymakers. The findings highlight how power dynamics shape AI development priorities, with only 28% of end-users feeling current transparency practices reflect their needs well, compared to 53% of builders and 49% of policymakers. This suggests that one-size-fits-all transparency solutions, such as AI disclosure and static documentation, may not serve diverse stakeholders, and calls for pluralistic transparency infrastructures. The study uses the Social Construction of Technology framework to show that transparency is situationally constructed: priorities shift with context and task structure, and role-specific frames activate when context demands them. The authors argue that meaningful AI transparency requires moving beyond current emphases on AI disclosure and static documentation toward pluralistic transparency infrastructures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Transparency has become a central principle in AI governance, but there is little agreement on what it means, for whom, and in what contexts. The Social Construction of Technology (SCOT) theory holds that human action shapes technology rather than the reverse, and interpretive flexibility refers to how different social groups assign different meanings to the same technological artifact. This paper applies SCOT to examine how stakeholders' interpretations of transparency influence its development and adoption in generative AI.

## [VTHarness: Agent Framework for Virtual Trial Protocol Operationalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05551) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced VirtualTrialHarness (VTHarness), an agent framework that coordinates virtual-trial operationalization by selecting reusable procedural skills for each workflow stage and using explicit contracts to maintain consistent study decisions across stages. They also introduced VTBenchmark for evaluating source selection and cohort construction, and across three complex synthetic environments VTHarness produced all required cohort outputs matching references in 95.1% of workflows after requirement clarification, compared with 60.4% using retrieval from the same procedural corpus. This work addresses a key bottleneck in virtual trials: translating study protocols into executable analyses that respect local data representations and operational conventions. If validated, such agent frameworks could reduce manual effort and errors in cohort construction, potentially accelerating evidence generation from routinely collected clinical data and benefiting clinical informatics teams and trial investigators. Procedural skills improved source recovery across three language models and increased source overlap with analyst code in 13 Mayo Clinic cases, while site-specific adaptation enabled reference-matched cohort execution across eight MIMIC-IV tasks. The framework also supported investigator review of cohort construction and endpoint analysis within the Mayo Clinic Virtual Trials Program, though evaluation relied on synthetic environments and a limited number of real-world cases.

Agent frameworks are LLM-based systems that can plan and execute multi-step tasks, and here they are combined with reusable procedural skills and explicit contracts to enforce consistency across workflow stages.

## [BrainBind maps synchronized EEG and fMRI to video embeddings for neural retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05581) ⭐️ 6.0/10

BrainBind introduces a framework that maps synchronized EEG and fMRI recordings during long-form naturalistic video viewing into frozen video embedding spaces, treating these spaces as common visual-semantic references for cross-modal neural retrieval. The paper also presents CineBrain, a new benchmark evaluated with a five-seed Recall@K 2x4 matrix across two video anchors and four system configurations, showing distinct retrieval profiles for different proxies and configurations. This work bridges two complementary neuroimaging modalities (EEG's high temporal resolution and fMRI's high spatial resolution) in a synchronized naturalistic setting, which could advance brain-computer interface and neural decoding research by enabling more robust cross-modal retrieval. It also provides a new benchmark (CineBrain) and open-source code, fostering reproducibility and future work in the neuro-AI community. The evaluation uses a five-seed Recall@K 2x4 matrix with two video anchors and four system configurations; with Omni, fMRI organizes the full candidate list most strongly (MRR = 23.82, nDCG@10 = 25.31), while Joint-Dual gives the highest mean label-aware R@1 (13.52%). An episode-disjoint evaluation quantifies the contribution of repeated stimulus identity and narrative context to the random split, and the code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: EEG and fMRI are two common neuroimaging techniques: EEG measures electrical brain activity with high temporal resolution but poor spatial resolution, while fMRI measures blood oxygenation changes with high spatial resolution but slow temporal resolution. Neural decoding aims to infer what a person is seeing or thinking from brain signals, often by aligning brain responses with computational models of stimuli. Recent work uses deep learning to map brain activity into embedding spaces of images or videos, but most studies use a single modality and static or short stimuli. Synchronized EEG-fMRI recording during naturalistic video viewing is challenging due to artifacts and the need for precise temporal alignment, but offers a richer, multimodal view of brain function.

A new arXiv paper quantifies the trait-state hierarchy in EEG embeddings from both signal processing and foundation models, finding that most features form a macro-trait hierarchy with micro-states within. The authors show this structure leads to suboptimal state distinguishability and propose normalization techniques that improve balanced accuracy for state classification by about 10%. This work highlights a fundamental limitation in current EEG foundation models for brain-computer interfaces: embeddings are dominated by subject-specific traits rather than task-relevant states. The proposed normalization and fine-tuning strategies could lead to more robust and generalizable BCI decoders, impacting both research and clinical applications. The analysis uses a hierarchy metric and kNN purity on BCI dataset trials, and linear probes reveal that the macro-trait hierarchy hinders state decoding. Fine-tuning models for downstream state decoding transforms embedding spaces from macro-trait to macro-state hierarchies, improving performance.

## [Relevance-Based Mixing of Feedback Fuzzy Cognitive Maps from Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05664) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper extends the legal theory of relevance to combine feedback fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) from multiple experts, introducing a Bayes-like theorem to de-mix the knowledge mixture and estimate the probative status of answers. It demonstrates the approach using synthetic FCMs generated from a 13-node expert FCM modeling ischemic-stroke risk. This work offers a neuro-symbolic method to selectively combine only relevant expert knowledge, which could improve accuracy and scalability as knowledge networks grow. It bridges legal evidence theory and AI knowledge fusion, potentially impacting expert systems and decision support in domains like healthcare. The approach compares equilibrium limit-cycle predictions of individual expert FCMs to those of the mixture FCM, using their difference to estimate probative status. The de-mixing process yields posterior-like FCMs and query-specific relevancy mixing weights, so different queries produce different relevancy-mixed FCMs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) are weighted directed graphs with cycles that represent causal relationships among concepts, often used to model complex systems based on expert opinion. Feedback FCMs are nonlinear dynamical systems whose iterative updates can simulate recursive causal mechanisms. In legal evidence theory, relevance requires that evidence be both probative (tending to prove or disprove a fact) and material (related to the case).

A new arXiv preprint (2611.05753) proposes a four-layer compositional knowledge architecture—Signals, Domains, Claims, and Dimensions—called a 'shared organizational cortex' that persists interpretations with justifications and standing, and uses a truth-maintenance network to revise standing as evidence changes. In a registered retrieval experiment, the Dimension term improved nDCG@10 by 0.028 (p = 0.002), and the full configuration improved nDCG@10 by 0.081 over Signals-only and 0.053 over graph-augmented Signals-only retrieval (both p < 0.001). If validated, it could improve retrieval-augmented generation and organizational decision-making by giving AI systems a persistent, auditable basis for shared judgement. The reported gains are modest—0.028 nDCG@10 for the Dimension term—and the work is a preprint without community discussion yet. The architecture relies on a truth-maintenance network and versioned Dimensions positioned on named axes, but the abstract does not detail dataset size, baselines, or implementation constraints.

## [EchoKV: Self-Corrective KV Cache Eviction via Iterative Query Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05903) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EchoKV introduces an iterative query-feedback approach to correct KV cache eviction errors, improving over LAQ on long-context tasks requiring dispersed evidence. It initializes a feedback-compatible bootstrap workspace using proposals at future decode positions, then uses early decode-generated queries to recover the workspace before the remaining lookahead steps. This work identifies query-workspace coupling as a practical failure mode of query-driven KV eviction, which can distort eviction decisions when evidence is dispersed across long contexts. The method selectively improves evidence-intensive tasks like multi-hop cross-document QA and long single-document comprehension, potentially benefiting LLM inference efficiency in long-context applications. EchoKV uses the same number of workspace forward passes as LAQ while adding a tensor re-scoring operation for workspace repair. On LongBench (16 tasks, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), it shows notable improvements over LAQ on several tasks requiring dispersed evidence, while maintaining comparable performance on the remaining subsets across budgets and backbones.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: KV cache stores key and value tensors for previously processed tokens to avoid recomputation during autoregressive generation, but its memory footprint grows with context length. KV cache eviction methods selectively discard less important tokens to reduce memory usage, and query-aware approaches like LAQ use lookahead queries to estimate token importance. LongBench is a bilingual, multitask benchmark for evaluating long-context understanding in LLMs.

The paper introduces BiSID-AVS, a method that uses binaural audio cues to disambiguate which same-class object is actually producing sound in audio-visual segmentation. It progressively refines category-level candidate regions into a mask of the true sounding instance by projecting interaural differences into a spatial response map and applying dual-level spatial conditioning. This work addresses a key limitation of existing audio-visual segmentation methods that fail when multiple same-class objects are present, improving instance-level accuracy by over 35 percentage points on benchmark subsets. It could benefit robotics and indoor perception systems that rely on identifying the correct sound source among similar objects. BiSID-AVS achieves improvements of 35.19 and 41.21 percentage points in instance-level mIoU on the binaurally extended AVS-Synthetic benchmark and the Habitat-AVS dataset, respectively, while substantially reducing false activation on non-sounding same-class regions. The method relies on binaural audio, so it assumes stereo audio input and may not apply to mono recordings.

## [CARM: Adaptive Conformal Risk Masking for Scientific Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06151) ⭐️ 6.0/10

CARM introduces a statistical framework that maintains experts with heterogeneous update rates and aggregates their threshold proposals online using an asymmetric surrogate loss to adaptively control spatial reconstruction risk under temporal distribution shift. On a daily South China Sea chlorophyll-a super-resolution stream, it reduces the empirical violation rate from 43.49% to 9.38% and the moving-average exceedance frequency from 47.29% to 1.22% at α=0.10, while retaining a non-trivial trusted region. This work addresses a critical gap in applying conformal risk control to real-world Earth-observation streams, where seasonality and episodic events cause fixed thresholds to become miscalibrated. It enables reliable spatial risk control for any super-resolution model that provides a spatial uncertainty score, potentially improving trust in downstream scientific analyses. CARM provides a theoretical guarantee that the time-averaged mask-conditioned risk converges to at most α at an O(1/T) rate without distributional assumptions and for any choice of aggregation weights across experts. All updates are causal and use only mature feedback available after a fixed delay, allowing operation with any SR model that provides a spatial uncertainty score.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: Conformal prediction is a framework that provides valid uncertainty quantification by constructing prediction sets with guaranteed coverage under the assumption of exchangeability. Conformal risk control extends this to control the expected value of any monotone loss function, but its guarantees rely on exchangeability, which is often violated in temporal data streams. Super-resolution in Earth observation aims to reconstruct fine-scale fields from coarse observations, and ensuring the reliability of these reconstructions is crucial for scientific applications.

## [DyPSI jointly generates physics fields and sensor trajectories via diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06280) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DyPSI introduces a joint diffusion framework that simultaneously generates future spatiotemporal physics fields and temporally coherent sensor trajectories from sparse historical observations. It lifts discrete sensor coordinates into continuous Sensor Position Fields, encodes both fields and sensing configurations with a shared Functional Tucker representation, and trains a joint diffusion model in the resulting latent space. This work addresses a key limitation of existing physics sensing methods that rely on static sensor layouts or frame-wise independent placement, enabling adaptive and temporally coherent sensing trajectories. It could improve data efficiency and reconstruction accuracy in applications like environmental monitoring and aerodynamic design under tight sensor budgets. DyPSI uses a Gaussian-process temporal kernel to correlate perturbations during diffusion training, biasing the denoiser toward temporally smooth recoveries, and provides an analysis linking the EDM training objective to a sequence-aggregated A-optimality criterion and a kernel-induced bound on discrete trajectory variation. Experiments on turbulent flow, GLORYS12 sea-surface temperature, and 3D car aerodynamics show consistent outperformance over static and frame-wise placement strategies, with substantial error reduction under tight sensor budgets.

## [MDP and Behavior Cloning Tackle Multi-Resident Data Association in Smart Homes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06296) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.06296) proposes reformulating multi-resident data association in smart homes as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) and solving it via Behavior Cloning (BC). The approach uses two architectures: a lightweight Transformer for multi-label classification and a fine-tuned LLM for conditional text generation, and it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on three datasets. This work addresses a critical challenge in ambient sensor-based activity recognition: maintaining global consistency in multi-resident environments, which could improve the reliability of smart home monitoring for elderly care and independent living. The use of behavior cloning avoids complex reward engineering, and the LLM-based policy shows preliminary cross-environment transfer potential. The Transformer treats association as multi-label classification over linearized event histories, while the fine-tuned LLM reformulates the problem as conditional text generation to leverage pre-trained semantic knowledge. Evaluations show significant improvements, especially in resolving concurrent multi-resident events, but the LLM's robustness in real deployments still requires further validation.

LegalEvolver introduces a rubric-guided self-evolution framework that optimizes retrieval queries to improve LLM-based legal judgment prediction. The method heuristically optimizes queries as seeds, summarizes rollout experience into rubrics, and iteratively performs rubric optimization and rubric-guided reinforcement learning to surpass the model's initial performance ceiling. This work addresses a known bottleneck in retrieval-augmented legal agents—poor query formulation—and shows that self-evolving rubrics can outperform fixed rubrics and direct SFT followed by RL. It could improve the reliability of AI-assisted legal analysis, where missing latent legal issues can lead to incorrect predictions. The framework relies on heuristic query seeds to make trajectory generation more efficient than unguided high-temperature rollouts, and it iteratively optimizes rubrics alongside rubric-guided RL. End-to-end benchmark results demonstrate the effectiveness of query optimization for LJP, though the contribution is a niche, incremental advance without broad impact signals.

MA-Mesh introduces a role-based self-healing layer for multi-agent systems, featuring cross-agent reflection and role-based failover to enhance resilience in dynamic environments. The framework generates resilience-aware system specifications and synthesizes them into executable multi-agent policy code, outperforming existing systems in tasks like software development, tool use, and scientific reasoning. It could lead to more reliable and adaptive AI systems in dynamic and uncertain environments, impacting areas like autonomous software development and complex problem-solving. The self-healing mechanisms include cross-agent reflection, where agents evaluate peers' performance for collective policy refinement, and role-based failover, which dynamically reassigns roles when sub-tasks fail. ElecLoad is a context-aware agent that adaptively organizes the electricity load forecasting process by combining historical load data with multiple event signals from web notices, weather alerts, and event schedules. It analyzes each event's relevance, timing, and interactions, and can either forecast the entire horizon directly or partition it into segments handled by sub-agents with selective refinement. Accurate load forecasting is critical for energy procurement, generation scheduling, and supply-demand balancing, and ElecLoad's event-aware approach addresses the complex interplay of real-world events that traditional methods often miss. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple datasets, offering a promising direction for integrating contextual signals into energy forecasting. ElecLoad is trained to assess event relevance, effect timing, and interactions, and when partitioning the horizon, sub-agents generate initial forecasts that the main agent reviews and guides selective refinement. Code is available at https://github.com/zhjai/ElecLoad, and experiments show state-of-the-art performance in most evaluation scenarios.

## [Formal analysis of diabetic retinopathy grading texts reveals gaps and ambiguities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06569) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper transcribes four published diabetic retinopathy grading texts into a rule language over 24 finding variables and exhaustively enumerates 1,425,807,360 finding profiles, using an SMT solver and OWL reasoner to show that every action-assigning text leaves some eyes without a category under every reading. It also finds that the 2017 tabulation has an empty proliferative class when read literally and a macular gap absent from the 2003 text, so no function maps 2003 grades onto 2017 grades. The findings highlight that published medical grading schemes contain ambiguities and gaps that readers must tacitly repair, which can lead to inconsistent interpretations and affect standardization and interoperability in health informatics. This has implications for clinical decision-making, AI training labels, and cross-document mapping in diabetic retinopathy screening. On 927 annotated photographs from three public datasets, assuming retinal thickening absent, the English text and the 2017 tabulation disagree on referral for 26.8% of eyes in the DDR dataset where both decide; a pre-registered experiment shows classifiers trained on each text's labels side with their own text. Additionally, language models without deliberation repaired most gap and overlap items even when told to read literally, while Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Claude Opus 5.5 reported defects far more often.

A new arXiv paper proposes a temporal organization layer for continual digital-life agents, using phase-tagged micro-epochs, duty-cycle control, and three nested clocks to coordinate busy/idle operations. It provides a projected duty-cycle update with deterministic descent guarantees and reports synthetic experiments (64 seeds each) showing 26.6% vs 11.1% success on an urgent/high-load subset when scheduling is decoupled. This work offers a formal, testable framework for temporal organization in continual agents, addressing how to interleave urgent interaction with background replay and safe long-term updates. It could inform the design of more robust agent architectures that balance responsiveness and stability, though its impact is currently limited to theoretical AI/ML research. The paper specifies coupled busy/idle execution contracts and a stochastic guarantee requiring an additional noise term, with the best tested gain ratio being 1.55 in the gain/noise model. A frozen-backbone Qwen3-8B prototype checks orchestration, but the authors caution that proxy outcomes are not task-benchmark evidence and do not imply machine consciousness or general-purpose agent superiority.

## [Reasoner-Checked Crosswalks for Information Exchange Standard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06628) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper formalizes crosswalk validation using coherence and conservativity checks, applies them to the Information Exchange Standard (IES) across thirteen ontologies, and shows that certification and human review catch different error types. Pairwise certification does not compose: merging three certified building crosswalks into a hub made five Brick classes unsatisfiable due to a homonym, while rejecting one row yielded a coherent hub entailing 290 new links. This work provides a rigorous, logic-based method for validating ontology crosswalks, which is crucial for ensuring the reliability of digital twin ecosystems and semantic web applications. It highlights that certification must be done at the hub level, not just pairwise, impacting how standards like IES are integrated with other ontologies. The method uses HermiT reasoner to check coherence and conservativity of merged ontologies. Certification alone removed all 526 conservativity violations of raw crosswalks, review alone left 73, and together they made eleven of twelve overlapping pairs coherent and conservative. The core hub is inconsistent before adding crosswalks because the Open Energy Ontology and BFO 2020 contradict each other.

A new arXiv paper proposes representing agent execution traces as LLM-generated structured summaries embedded with a standard text encoder, then using these representations to predict a system's full benchmark score from just a few task executions. On SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0, augmenting an item-response model with this geometry-based prediction reduced mean absolute error from 0.13 to 0.08 and from 0.14 to 0.12, respectively, when given a single random executed task. Efficient benchmarking is increasingly important as agent evaluations become expensive, and this work shows that rich execution traces contain signal beyond binary correctness. By reducing system-identity bias, the approach could make small-sample agent comparisons more reliable and fairer across model families and harnesses. The method extracts structured summaries with an LLM and embeds them with a standard text encoder to preserve correctness- and task-related signal while minimizing system identity, model family, and harness signals that dominate raw trace embeddings. The gains are statistically significant (Wilcoxon p < 10^-4 and p < 10^-3), and the approach dominates baselines for any given number of executed tasks when full reference systems are available.

The paper introduces Generalized Belief Propagation Network (GBPNet), a neural decoder that preserves message-passing symmetry by using cascaded variable-node updates and learned element-wise message scaling while retaining the Tanner graph structure. It also proposes progressive training and high-SNR training strategies, and experiments on several linear codes show lower bit error rates than conventional belief propagation and state-of-the-art neural decoders. Neural decoders have recently shown performance gains over classical iterative algorithms, and GBPNet's architecture and training strategies offer a new approach to improving decoding accuracy for error-correcting codes. This could benefit communication systems that rely on reliable data transmission over noisy channels, though as a specialized arXiv preprint its immediate industry impact is limited. GBPNet retains the Tanner graph structure and uses cascaded variable-node updates with learned element-wise message scaling to preserve message-passing symmetry. The two training strategies, progressive training and high-SNR training, are designed to improve decoding accuracy, but the paper does not specify the exact codes tested or the SNR ranges used.

Neural decoders replace or augment parts of BP with learned components to improve performance, but they must respect the symmetry of message passing to remain effective. Generalized belief propagation extends BP to more complex graphical models, and this paper combines it with neural networks.

## [AI exposure linked to less dull, dirty, dangerous work, study finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06888) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.06888) analyzes 21 O*NET items across 754 US occupations and finds that higher AI exposure is associated with lower dull, dirty, and dangerous (DDD) condition scores under both an LLM-based measure and the AI Occupational Exposure (AIOE) measure. However, after adjusting for physical demands, education, training, experience, and wages, the association weakens and becomes statistically insignificant for LLM exposure. The study challenges the common narrative that AI exposure primarily targets dull, dirty, and dangerous jobs, suggesting that the relationship is more nuanced and sensitive to occupational characteristics. This has implications for how policymakers and researchers interpret AI exposure measures and design interventions for workers. The analysis uses 21 O*NET items to construct DDD scores and compares two AI exposure measures: an LLM-based measure and AIOE. Adjusting for occupational characteristics reduces the composite association, with confidence intervals including zero for LLM exposure but remaining below zero for AIOE, and separating repetition from restricted discretion alters the interpretation of the combined Dull score.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 5, 05:00

**Background**: O*NET is a free online database containing hundreds of standardized job definitions and skill requirements for US occupations. AI Occupational Exposure (AIOE) is a validated measure that scores an occupation's exposure to AI based on its task content, while LLM-based exposure measures assess how much an occupation's tasks can be performed by large language models. The study examines whether occupations with higher AI exposure are indeed those with more dull, dirty, and dangerous working conditions, a common justification for automation.
